## C4. Direction 03 komanda yoldaşının araşdırma sualı (introvert / sosial səyahət)
oktyabr əvvəli · mənbə: 2c79bf8e-attachment.txt
1. Research Question
Core question: How can a vehicle serve passengers who don't travel the same way — some wanting rich, social, shared experiences, others needing quiet and solitude — without forcing everyone into one fixed mode?
This question grew directly out of two separate threads of research that turned out to point at the same underlying problem. My first thread (BMW's theatre screen vs. Hyundai's control screen + Bluetooth) explored how richness gets created during travel time — either curated in advance, or emerging spontaneously once friction is removed. My second thread, sparked by my own sketch, asked a related but distinct question: what happens when the people in the car don't agree on what "richness" even means for them right now? One person's idea of a great drive is a shared screen and music. Another's is total quiet. Current vehicle design assumes everyone in the cabin wants the same thing at the same time — and neither the BMW nor the Hyundai approach actually solves that.
2. Background
Social: long drives and shared travel are common, but the people sharing them are rarely socially uniform — introverts, extroverts, people who are easily overstimulated, and people who crave connection are often in the same vehicle at once.
Technological: autonomous and semi-autonomous driving is freeing up cabin design from being purely control-oriented, opening space for entertainment, social configuration, and personal retreat — but most current concepts still design one fixed interior mode.
Cultural: at the same time, "always-on" digital culture has raised the expectation that any travel time should be usable — but usable doesn't mean the same thing to everyone.
Why it matters: current concepts treat this as a content problem (add a screen, add sound) rather than a social problem (people in the same space needing incompatible things at the same time).
3. Key Findings
Finding 1 (screen thread): BMW's 31.3" 8K rear display manufactures richness in advance — curated, screen-first, passive. Hyundai's plain control screen, paired with an easy Bluetooth speaker, let richness emerge spontaneously without any content being designed for it.
Finding 2 (screen thread): The large windows and open cabin space in the concept models offered a third, quieter form of richness — just observing the surroundings — that no current concept treats as a designed feature.
Finding 3 (pod thread): Existing "flexible cabin" concepts (Audi urbansphere, M.Vision TO/HI, Audi activesphere) all reconfigure the interior of a single shared cabin — swivel seats, privacy screens, mode switches — but none of them physically separate passengers into independent spaces. Audi's own Capsule concept goes the opposite direction: full separation, but only ever for one person, permanently.
4. Insight / Tension
Two extremes currently exist in the research: fully shared cabins that try to soften disagreement with screens and privacy dividers (urbansphere), and fully solo capsules that avoid the disagreement entirely by removing other people (Audi Capsule). Neither actually resolves the core tension — that the same trip, with the same people, can require both social and solitary modes, sometimes within the same hour. Richness (my first thread) and social comfort (my second thread) turn out to be the same underlying issue: current vehicles pick one fixed experience and ask every passenger to adapt to it, instead of letting the vehicle adapt to each person.
5. Design Opportunity
There's an opportunity to design a vehicle that doesn't commit to one social configuration for the whole trip — one that can be fully shared (screen on, speaker connected, everyone together) or fully individual (quiet, private, screen off) and can shift between the two on demand, based on what the actual people inside need in that moment — not what the vehicle was designed to assume they'd want.
DESIGN PROPOSAL — Concept Direction
6. Concept Direction
Concept statement: A modular, disconnectable vehicle made of independent pods that can join into one connected group cabin (shared screen, shared sound, conversation) or separate into individual private pods (quiet, no shared content, personal space) — chosen by the passengers, not fixed by the vehicle.
Target user: groups traveling together with mixed social needs — families, friend groups, coworkers — where not everyone wants the same experience at the same time.
Context: road trips, group travel, any situation where an introverted or overstimulated passenger needs to opt out of the group experience temporarily without ending the trip for everyone else.
Key interaction: pods physically connect/disconnect; the shared screen (styled after the BMW/Hyundai comparison — Netflix in my sketch) only activates when pods are joined, disappearing entirely in solo mode.
Why this is different from what already exists:
Audi urbansphere softens social friction with swivel seats and privacy screens, but everyone is still in the same shared cabin — you can turn away from someone, but you can't leave the space.
Audi Capsule solves solo travel completely, but permanently — there's no version of it that becomes a group space when needed. It picks one social mode at the design stage and locks it in.
My concept doesn't pick a side. It treats "together" and "apart" as two states of the same vehicle, switchable in the moment, which is what real groups actually need — not a compromise between social and solo, but the ability to be fully one or the other, on demand.
Why this is realistic to build, not just a sketch:
Audi and Italdesign have already tested autonomous docking between a passenger capsule and a separate driving module — the hardest-sounding part of this idea (precise, automated pod-to-frame connection) already has a working precedent. Electric drivetrains make modular pods more feasible too, since each pod only needs a compact motor and battery rather than sharing one continuous chassis. And the broader industry is already moving toward reconfigurable, purpose-flexible platforms — Toyota's e-Palette and Audi's activesphere are built on the same core belief, that a single vehicle shouldn't have to lock users into one fixed use.
Theoretical grounding:
Proxemic Interaction (Ballendat, Marquardt & Greenberg, 2010) — most systems sense and respond to social distance automatically; my concept makes proximity a direct, physical choice instead — connecting or disconnecting is a way of setting a social boundary yourself.
Getting a Grip on Tangible Interaction (Hornecker & Buur, 2006) — argues that physical space itself shapes social interaction, not just interfaces layered on top of it. There's no app or button mediating group vs. solo mode here — the vehicle's shape is the interaction.
Moving Methods, Travelling Times (Watts & Urry, 2008) — ties back to my first thread: richness during travel doesn't have to be manufactured (BMW) or purely emergent (Hyundai) — it can be a structural choice built into the vehicle itself.
7. Scenario (plan-level — panels to sketch next)
A group of friends start a road trip; pods connected, shared screen playing content, music on.
One passenger starts to feel overstimulated and signals they want quiet.
Their pod detaches — screen and shared audio turn off, cabin becomes private.
The rest of the group continues, connected, watching/talking.
Later, at a stop, pods reconnect for a shared meal/conversation.
Arrival — everyone got the kind of trip they personally needed, without anyone having to leave the group entirely or force everyone else into their preferred mode.
8. Sketch Exploration
Planned for next week — variations on pod shape, connection/docking mechanism, interface states (connected vs. solo), and touchpoints for signaling a mode change (gesture, button, app).
9. System / Interaction Diagram
User (signals desire to connect/disconnect) → Interface (confirms mode change) → Mobility/pod (physically docks or detaches) → AI/System (reconfigures shared screen/audio state) → Environment (lighting, sound zone shifts per pod) → Feedback (visual/haptic confirmation of new state).
10. Proposed Outcome + Next Prototype
First prototype (sketch only at this stage): the hand-drawn concept sketch, to communicate the core idea before building anything physical.

## C5. Direction 03: Yuna personası, versiya 1
oktyabr əvvəli · mənbə: 8b43cfb7-attachment.txt
1. Persona
Yuna, 19 — a newly licensed driver (holds her license for about 6 months). She's naturally introverted and gets easily distracted by conversation, music, and sudden noise, especially when she needs to concentrate. She cares deeply about driving safely, but as a new and somewhat anxious driver, she doesn't feel comfortable asking her family to stay quiet — she doesn't want to seem like she "can't handle it."
2. The Critical Moment
Yuna is driving her family on a road trip. As the car enters a busy highway, she needs full concentration to merge safely and keep up with faster traffic. In the back seat, her siblings keep chatting and laughing. She doesn't want to ask them to stop — it would make her feel embarrassed, or worse, worried — so she tries to focus through the noise instead of asking for silence.
3. Today's Solution — and Why It Fails
Partition systems already exist in some vehicles — most visibly in limousines and taxis, where a solid divider separates the driver from passengers. But these solve a different problem: privacy between a driver and strangers, not sound management between family members who want to stay connected most of the time. These partitions are typically all-or-nothing — fully open or fully closed — cutting off all communication and audio rather than selectively managing noise. They're also built for commercial vehicles, not something realistically retrofitted into a personal family car for occasional use during specific moments, like a highway merge.
4. System Response
Instead of a physical wall or full separation, the system uses directional sound zones — similar to Hyundai's Separated Sound Zone technology — to quiet the driver's immediate space while passengers continue talking normally in the rest of the cabin. When the driver needs to focus, their zone becomes acoustically isolated without physically cutting them off from the car or the people in it. This is a more realistic, buildable version of the original capsule idea — it achieves the same goal (letting the driver step out of the social environment without truly leaving it) using existing, real automotive audio technology rather than a mechanical separation system.
5. Decision Logic
* If the driver activates focus mode, their sound zone takes priority — passengers can keep talking or playing music in their own zone, but it won't reach the driver's zone.
* The driver can enable or disable focus mode at any time via a simple control (see Touchpoints). Passengers cannot turn it off from their seats, since the entire point is to protect the driver's concentration without requiring a negotiation.
* Safety override: focus mode never fully blocks sound — it reduces passenger conversation/music volume in the driver's zone rather than eliminating all audio, so the driver can still hear something unusual (a child crying, a raised or alarmed voice) even while concentrating. True emergency-level sounds (shouting, distress) are not filtered out.
6. Touchpoints
* Activation: a single button on the steering wheel or center console, so the driver can turn focus mode on/off without taking their eyes off the road.
* System feedback to driver: a subtle ambient light on the dashboard (e.g., a soft blue glow) confirms focus mode is active.
* System feedback to passengers: a small light indicator near the rear seats (or a soft chime) lets passengers know the driver has entered focus mode, so they understand why their audio suddenly feels "separated," without needing to be told directly.
* Automatic trigger: the system can also suggest focus mode when entering a highway or high-speed road (based on GPS/speed data), but only as a prompt — not an automatic action.
7. Over-Intervention Check
* The system never activates focus mode fully automatically — on a highway, it suggests turning it on (a brief visual/audio prompt), but the driver must confirm with one tap. This avoids the system making a decision the driver didn't choose.
* The driver can disable focus mode at any time with the same single button — no multi-step process.
* To avoid complicated setup, there are no custom settings to configure before a trip; focus mode works the same way by default every time, so there's nothing to "remember" to set up.
8. Scenario (storyboard, 5 frames)
1. Situation — Yuna, a new driver, starts a family road trip. Passengers are chatting comfortably in the back.
2. Context change — The car enters a busy highway; a dashboard prompt suggests focus mode as traffic speed increases.
3. System detects / driver acts — Yuna taps the steering wheel control to activate focus mode.
4. System responds — Her seating zone goes quiet while passengers continue talking normally in their own zone; a small light near the back seats shows the group that focus mode is on.
5. Result — Yuna merges and drives through the highway calmly and focused, without needing to ask anyone to be quiet; her family continues their conversation undisturbed.
(Suggest mixing a wide cabin shot for frames 1 and 5 with close-ups on the steering wheel control and the sound-zone light for frames 3–4, with one caption sentence under each.)
9. Open Risks
* If the driver becomes too reliant on focus mode, could they lose the habit of managing real-world distraction without it?
* How does the system handle a genuine emergency sound that's quiet but still urgent (e.g., a passenger feeling sick but speaking softly)?
* Passengers might feel subtly "shut out" even though they're not required to be quiet — this needs user testing to confirm it doesn't feel isolating or exclusionary.
Source note: 
"A University of Iowa / AAA Foundation for Traffic Safety study analyzing nearly 1,700 in-vehicle crash videos from teen drivers aged 16–19 found that in about 40% of crashes where passengers were present, the driver was looking at or talking to a passenger in the six seconds before the crash. The risk also scaled with group size — 54% of crashes involved one passenger, 74% involved two or more." This lines up directly with Yuna's profile as a new, young driver.

## C6. Direction 03: Yuna personası, versiya 2
oktyabr əvvəli · mənbə: 9228c378-attachment.txt
1. Persona
Yuna, 19 — a newly licensed driver (around 6 months of experience). She's introverted and easily distracted by conversation, music, and sudden noise, especially when she needs to focus. She cares deeply about driving safely, but as a new driver, she doesn't feel comfortable asking her family to be quiet — she doesn't want to seem like she "can't handle it" behind the wheel.
2. The Critical Moment
Yuna is driving her family on a road trip. As the car enters a busy highway, she needs full concentration to merge and keep pace with faster traffic. In the back seat, her siblings keep talking and laughing. She doesn't want to ask them to stop, so she tries to power through the distraction instead of addressing it.
3. Today's Solution — and Why It Fails
Partition systems already exist — most visibly in limousines and taxis, where a solid divider separates the driver from the back seat. But even these don't fully solve the problem: the front passenger seat still has direct, unblocked access to the driver, so a distracting conversation partner sitting right next to the driver isn't addressed at all. These partitions also solve a different problem to begin with — privacy between a driver and strangers — not noise management between family members who still want to stay connected for most of the ride.
The other existing direction is removing the driver entirely — fully autonomous vehicles like Waymo. But full self-driving still requires near-universal adoption and infrastructure to be reliably safe, and that kind of large-scale shift doesn't happen quickly. A solution that keeps a human driver in control, but gives them a way to manage distraction directly, is realistic sooner.
Beyond the driver's focus problem, families also run into a second, related issue: needing to split up and go to different destinations. Today, that means taking a second vehicle, putting someone on public transport, or driving everyone around in a fixed sequence of drop-offs.
4. System Response
The vehicle is made of connected capsules, and whether they're joined or split is decided once, at the start of the trip — not mid-ride. If everyone is headed to the same place, the capsules travel connected as one vehicle. If passengers have different destinations from the outset, their capsules can split apart before departure, so each person travels their own route without needing a second car.
Once a ride is underway with everyone traveling together, a separate mechanism handles in-ride distraction: a window can close around an individual seat — including the driver's — cutting off conversation and noise from that seat without physically splitting the vehicle. The driver's capsule never detaches, since they're still the one driving; closing their window simply gives them the same acoustic separation a limousine partition gives the back seat, but extended to cover the front seat too.
5. Decision Logic
* Driver's window vs. passenger activity: if the driver closes their window, it takes priority — passengers can keep talking or playing music in the rest of the cabin, but it won't reach the driver.
* A passenger wanting quiet (e.g., to sleep): any passenger can close their own seat's window independently, without needing the driver's or anyone else's permission.
* Who can open a closed window: only the person inside that seat controls it — a passenger can't reopen the driver's window, and the driver can't reopen a passenger's window from the front.
* Safety check: a closed window never fully seals out sound. Normal conversation and music are blocked, but loud or distressed sounds (a child crying hard, shouting, alarms) still pass through, so the driver can never be fully unaware of something urgent happening in the back.
6. Touchpoints
* Activation: a single button on the steering wheel or console lets the driver close their window without taking their hands off the wheel for more than a tap. Passengers have the same single-button control at their own seat.
* Driver feedback: a soft light on the dashboard confirms the driver's window is closed.
* Passenger feedback: a small indicator light near each seat shows whether that seat's window is open or closed, so the rest of the group can see at a glance who's currently separated.
* Destination split (pre-trip only): before departure, passengers select their destination on a shared trip screen; if destinations differ, the system shows which capsules will split and confirms with each person before the trip starts.
7. Over-Intervention Check
* The system never closes a window automatically on its own — entering a highway only triggers a brief suggestion (a light prompt) for the driver, who must confirm with one tap to actually close it.
* Any window can be reopened at any time by the person in that seat, with the same single control — no multi-step process.
* No setup or configuration is needed before a ride for window control; it behaves the same way by default every time. The only pre-trip decision is destination splitting, which only appears if destinations actually differ.
8. Scenario (5 frames)
1. Situation — Yuna, a new driver, starts a family road trip; everyone's capsules are connected, passengers chatting normally.
2. Context change — The car enters a busy highway; a dashboard prompt suggests closing her window as speed and traffic increase.
3. System detects / driver acts — Yuna taps the steering wheel control to close her window.
4. System responds — Her seat's window closes, cutting off the conversation from the rest of the cabin; a light near the back seats shows the group that her window is now closed.
5. Result — Yuna merges and drives the highway calmly, without needing to ask anyone to be quiet; her family continues talking normally, and she reopens her window once the highway stretch is behind her.
9. Open Risks
* If window-closing is used too often, could the driver lose the habit of handling normal in-car distraction without it?
* How is a genuinely urgent but quiet signal (e.g., a passenger feeling sick, speaking softly) still detected through the emergency sound channel?
* Does a visibly closed window make passengers feel shut out, even though they're not required to be quiet themselves? This needs real user testing to confirm it doesn't read as exclusionary.
* For the pre-trip destination split: what happens if someone changes their mind about their destination mid-route, since the system isn't designed to reconfigure once the trip has started?
A University of Iowa / AAA Foundation for Traffic Safety study analyzing nearly 1,700 in-vehicle crash videos from teen drivers aged 16–19 found that in about 40% of crashes where passengers were present, the driver was looking at or talking to a passenger in the six seconds before the crash. The risk also scaled with group size — 54% of crashes involved one passenger, 74% involved two or more.

## C7. Direction 02: Motion Comfort While Driving (araşdırma)
oktyabr əvvəli · mənbə: 38359b79-attachment.txt
Motion Comfort While Driving
1. Main Issue — Motion Sickness
As vehicles become more autonomous, passengers will be able to use travel time for activities such as working, reading, watching videos, or using their phones.
However, these activities can increase the risk of motion sickness because visual information from a stationary screen may conflict with the movement sensed by the body and vestibular system.
Motion sickness can develop gradually rather than appearing immediately. This makes it difficult for a passenger to recognize the problem before the symptoms become strong.
Our concept focuses on detecting the risk of motion sickness before severe discomfort occurs and adapting the vehicle experience to the individual passenger.
⸻
2. Persona 1 — Alina
Alina, 20, Design Student
Alina frequently uses autonomous vehicles to travel between university and home.
She often uses the travel time to work on presentations and design projects.
She is relatively sensitive to motion sickness, especially when looking at a screen during turns.
Goal
Finish her work during the journey without having to stop because of motion sickness.
⸻
3. Critical Moment
Alina has an important presentation at university.
She gets into an autonomous vehicle and opens her tablet because she needs to finish the final slides before arriving.
At first, the ride is smooth and she can work normally.
The vehicle then enters a winding road with repeated turns.
Alina continues looking at her tablet because she needs to finish the presentation.
The vehicle detects:
* Repeated upcoming turns
* Alina’s gaze fixed on the tablet
* Her individual motion sensitivity
* Changes in head movement
* Changes in posture
The estimated motion sickness risk increases.
Without the System
Alina begins to feel dizzy and nauseous.
She has to:
Stop working → close the tablet → look outside → wait for symptoms to decrease
If the symptoms become severe, she may need to take motion sickness medication.
The problem is not simply that the ride is uncomfortable.
The vehicle has prevented Alina from using autonomous travel time for the activity she needed to complete.
⸻
4. Why Is Our Solution Better Than Today?
Current Experience
Looking at tablet
↓
Winding road
↓
Sensory conflict
↓
Motion sickness
↓
Stop working
The passenger has to change their own behavior after discomfort has already started.
Proposed Experience
Looking at tablet
↓
Winding road predicted
↓
Motion sickness risk detected
↓
Minimum necessary intervention
↓
Passenger remains comfortable
↓
Continues working
The key difference is that the system responds before discomfort becomes severe, rather than requiring the passenger to react afterward.
The goal is not to eliminate all movement.
The goal is to help the passenger adapt to movement while continuing their activity.
⸻
5. System Logic
The system combines multiple signals.
Vehicle Movement
* Speed
* Acceleration
* Braking
* Steering
* Road curvature
* Upcoming turns
Passenger State
* Eye gaze
* Head movement
* Posture
* Seat pressure distribution
Personal Profile
* Previous motion sensitivity
* Previous reactions to specific movements
* Preferred intervention level
These inputs are combined to estimate motion sickness risk, not to diagnose motion sickness.
System Flow
Vehicle Movement + Passenger State + Personal Profile
↓
Estimate Motion Sickness Risk
↓
LOW / MEDIUM / HIGH
↓
Select Minimum Necessary Intervention
↓
Adapt Passenger Experience
⸻
6. Risk Levels
The system uses the principle of minimum necessary intervention.
LOW RISK
The system detects no significant risk.
Response:
No intervention.
Alina continues normally.
⸻
MEDIUM RISK
The system predicts a moderate possibility of discomfort.
Response:
* Slight lateral seat support
* Subtle tactile cue before significant turns
The intervention remains almost unnoticeable.
⸻
HIGH RISK
The system predicts a high probability of discomfort.
Response:
* Stronger lateral seat support
* Earlier preparation before turns
* Smoother acceleration and braking
* Earlier or stronger tactile cues
The system increases intervention only when necessary.
⸻
7. Touchpoint — Tactile Cue
The tactile cue should not feel like an alarm.
A short, subtle vibration can be delivered through the side bolster of the seat, shortly before a predicted turn.
For example:
Upcoming left turn → subtle vibration on left side bolster
The cue gives the passenger a physical preview of the upcoming movement.
The passenger does not need to look away from the tablet to understand that movement is about to occur.
User Control
Alina can:
* Turn tactile feedback off
* Reduce its intensity
* Change the intervention level
* Allow the system to adapt automatically
The system should never force a personalized intervention on the passenger.
⸻
8. Vehicle-Level vs. Seat-Level
This distinction is central to the concept.
Seat-Level Adaptation
Can be personalized for each passenger:
* Lateral support
* Lumbar support
* Headrest
* Tactile feedback
This is the first level of intervention because it affects only the individual passenger.
Vehicle-Level Adaptation
Affects everyone:
* Acceleration
* Braking
* Turning
* Driving smoothness
Vehicle-level intervention should only be used when necessary.
Principle
Personal problem → seat-level solution first.
Vehicle-wide risk → vehicle-level adaptation.
⸻
9. Passenger Conflict
Alina is highly sensitive to motion.
Another passenger, Leo, is not sensitive to motion sickness and prefers a faster, more dynamic ride.
Conflict
Alina: Needs smoother movement.
Leo: Wants the vehicle to maintain its normal driving style.
Decision Logic
The system prioritizes seat-level intervention first.
For example:
Alina’s seat → increased lateral support + tactile cue
Leo’s seat → unchanged
The vehicle does not slow down simply because Alina has a higher sensitivity.
If Alina’s risk becomes severe, the system can apply a limited vehicle-level adjustment.
The goal is to find the minimum change that protects the sensitive passenger without unnecessarily affecting everyone else.
Priority
Safety → Individual intervention → Shared vehicle adaptation
⸻
10. Persona 2 — Emma
Emma, 9, Elementary School Student
Emma frequently experiences motion sickness but has difficulty recognizing or explaining her symptoms.
Situation
Emma is sitting in the back seat during a long family trip.
She is watching a tablet while the vehicle enters a winding road.
She cannot clearly tell her parents that she is beginning to feel uncomfortable.
System
The vehicle detects:
* Looking down at a screen
* Repeated head movement
* Posture changes
* Upcoming turns
The system estimates increased risk and provides gentle seat support and tactile feedback.
Goal
Detect discomfort early without requiring Emma to explain how she feels.
⸻
11. Persona 3 — Leo
Leo, 25, Motion-Insensitive Passenger
Leo rarely experiences motion sickness.
He does not need additional motion support.
Situation
Leo shares a vehicle with Alina.
Alina’s motion sickness risk increases during a winding road.
System
Alina’s seat:
Increased lateral support + tactile cue
Leo’s seat:
No intervention
This demonstrates that personalization does not mean applying the same experience to everyone.
⸻
12. Passenger Recognition
The vehicle needs to know who is sitting in each seat to apply the correct personal motion profile.
Possible recognition methods include:
* Smartphone
* NFC
* Personal vehicle account
* Seat occupancy + user profile
* Optional biometric recognition
Interaction
Passenger enters
↓
Passenger is recognized
↓
Personal motion profile loaded
↓
System monitors current context
↓
Personalized intervention
Recognition should happen automatically with minimal interaction.
⸻
13. Privacy — Open Risk
The system may use an interior camera to estimate:
* Eye gaze
* Head movement
* Posture
This creates a privacy concern.
The system should:
* Minimize collected data
* Process sensitive information locally where possible
* Clearly communicate what is being detected
* Allow passengers to understand or control sensing
Open Question
How can the vehicle provide human-aware adaptation without making passengers feel constantly monitored?
⸻
14. Evidence — Open Research Question
Existing motion sickness research supports the importance of sensory conflict and the relationship between visual information and vehicle motion.
However, the effectiveness of anticipatory tactile cues and adaptive seat support specifically for reducing motion sickness in autonomous vehicles needs further investigation.
Research direction:
“anticipatory cues motion sickness automated vehicles”
Potential evidence should examine whether:
* Anticipatory cues reduce sensory conflict
* Predictive information improves motion comfort
* Physical seat support reduces unwanted body movement
* Combining vehicle prediction with passenger-specific intervention improves comfort
If sufficient evidence cannot be found, the effectiveness of these interventions should remain an open research risk rather than being presented as a proven fact.
⸻
15. Storyboard — Alina
Frame 1 — Situation
Wide shot: cabin
Alina sits in the autonomous vehicle and starts working on her tablet.
Caption:
“Alina uses the travel time to finish her presentation.”
⸻
Frame 2 — Context Change
Wide shot: vehicle + road
The vehicle approaches a winding road.
Caption:
“The vehicle predicts a series of upcoming turns.”
⸻
Frame 3 — System Detects
Close-up: Alina + tablet / system visualization
Her gaze remains on the tablet while the vehicle predicts repeated turns.
Caption:
“Gaze on screen + upcoming turns → motion sickness risk increases.”
⸻
Frame 4 — System Responds
Close-up: seat bolster
The side bolster slightly adjusts and a subtle tactile cue activates before the turn.
Caption:
“The seat prepares Alina’s body before the movement occurs.”
⸻
Frame 5 — User Action
Medium shot: Alina working
Alina continues working without looking away from the tablet.
Caption:
“Alina continues working because the intervention does not interrupt her.”
⸻
Frame 6 — Result
Wide shot: vehicle arriving at university
Alina finishes her slides.
Caption:
“Alina completes her work without needing to stop because of motion discomfort.”
⸻
16. Storyboard — Emma
Frame 1
Emma watches a tablet in the back seat.
“Emma watches a video during a family trip.”
Frame 2
The vehicle approaches a winding road.
“The vehicle predicts repeated turns.”
Frame 3
The system detects gaze, head movement and posture changes.
“Motion sickness risk increases.”
Frame 4
The seat provides gentle support.
“The system intervenes before Emma becomes severely uncomfortable.”
Frame 5
Emma continues watching comfortably.
“Emma does not need to explain her discomfort.”
Frame 6
The vehicle leaves the winding road.
“Early intervention helps maintain her comfort.”
⸻
17. Storyboard — Leo
Frame 1
Leo and Alina share the vehicle.
“Two passengers with different motion sensitivities share the same ride.”
Frame 2
The vehicle approaches a winding road.
“Alina’s risk begins to increase.”
Frame 3
The system compares their individual profiles.
“Alina requires intervention while Leo does not.”
Frame 4
Alina’s seat adjusts.
“Alina receives personalized support.”
Frame 5
Leo’s seat remains unchanged.
“Leo’s experience is not unnecessarily modified.”
Frame 6
Both continue their journey.
“Individual needs are balanced within one shared vehicle.”
⸻
18. Sketch Exploration — 15–20 Sketches
Tactile Interaction
1. Side bolster vibration
2. Seat cushion vibration
3. Lumbar vibration
4. Headrest vibration
5. Different vibration patterns
Seat Support
6. Side bolster adjustment
7. Lumbar adjustment
8. Headrest adjustment
9. Full seat posture adjustment
Interface
10. Risk level shown on display
11. Risk level hidden from passenger
12. ON/OFF control
13. Intervention intensity control
14. Personal sensitivity settings
Multi-Passenger
15. Top-down cabin with two passengers
16. Different seat responses
17. Vehicle-level vs. seat-level adaptation
18. Passenger recognition
Privacy
19. Camera sensing visualization
20. Privacy control / sensing OFF state
Each sketch should be quick and exploratory, approximately 5–10 minutes per concept, rather than polished final drawings.
⸻
Core Concept
Human-Aware Mobility
The vehicle does not simply respond to the road.
It understands the relationship between:
Passenger + Seat + Movement + Context
and adapts the experience before discomfort becomes severe.
Sense → Predict → Prepare → Adapt
From Road-Aware Mobility → Human-Aware Mobility

## C8. Direction 02: Motion Comfort, təqdimat skripti
oktyabr əvvəli · mənbə: 5a61e69c-attachment.txt
1. Introduction
Now, I’m going to present our concept, Motion Comfort While Driving.
As vehicles become more autonomous, passengers will have more freedom to use their travel time. They can work, study, watch videos, use their phones, or simply relax.
However, even if the vehicle is autonomous, the human body still experiences acceleration, braking, turning, and road movement.
This can create motion sickness, especially when the passenger is looking at a screen.
Our question is: How can a vehicle understand the passenger’s body and prepare them for movement before motion sickness becomes severe?
⸻
2. The Problem
Motion sickness can happen when the information received by our eyes does not match what our inner ear and body sense.
For example, when a passenger looks at a stable tablet screen, their eyes see a stable image, while their body feels the vehicle turning or accelerating.
This sensory mismatch can cause nausea, dizziness, headache, sweating, and discomfort.
The problem is that passengers often notice these symptoms only after they have already started feeling uncomfortable.
So instead of waiting for the passenger to react, we propose that the vehicle should predict the risk and respond in advance.
⸻
3. Persona — Alina
Our main persona is Alina, a 20-year-old design student.
She often uses autonomous vehicles between university and home, and she likes to use travel time to work on presentations and design projects.
However, she is relatively sensitive to motion sickness, especially when she looks at a screen during a ride.
For Alina, travel time could be useful working time, but motion sickness can suddenly interrupt her work.
⸻
4. Critical Moment
Here is one critical moment.
Alina has an important presentation to finish, so she opens her tablet and starts working during the ride.
The vehicle is approaching a winding road with repeated turns.
The system detects that Alina is looking at her tablet, while the vehicle is about to make several turns.
Her head movement and posture also indicate that she may be affected by the upcoming movement.
The system estimates that her motion-sickness risk is increasing.
Instead of waiting until Alina feels sick, the vehicle prepares her body before the movement happens.
⸻
5. Why Is This Better Than Today?
Today, if passengers start feeling motion sick, they usually have to change their own behavior.
They may close their tablet, stop reading, look outside the window, or take medicine.
But these solutions happen after discomfort has already started.
Our concept changes this interaction.
Instead of asking the passenger to adapt to the vehicle, the vehicle adapts to the passenger.
The goal is not to completely eliminate movement.
The goal is to make the movement more predictable and easier for the human body to understand.
⸻
6. System Logic
The system combines three types of information.
First, vehicle movement, such as speed, acceleration, braking, steering, upcoming turns, and road conditions.
Second, passenger state, such as eye gaze, head movement, posture, and seat pressure.
Third, the passenger’s personal motion profile, which can contain information about their previous sensitivity.
The system combines these signals to estimate the probability of motion sickness.
It does not diagnose the passenger.
It simply asks: How likely is this passenger to feel uncomfortable during the upcoming movement?
Then the system responds accordingly.
⸻
7. Risk Levels
We designed three levels of intervention.
At low risk, the vehicle does nothing. The passenger can continue normally.
At medium risk, the system gives a subtle intervention. For example, the side bolster of the seat provides slight lateral support, and a small tactile cue warns the passenger about the upcoming movement.
At high risk, the intervention becomes stronger. The seat provides more support, the vehicle can prepare acceleration and braking earlier, and the tactile cue can become slightly stronger or earlier.
The main principle is minimum necessary intervention.
The system should not constantly interfere with the passenger.
It should only intervene when it is necessary.
⸻
8. Tactile Cue
One important interaction is the tactile cue.
For example, if the vehicle predicts an upcoming left turn, the left side of the seat can give Alina a short, subtle vibration before the turn.
This gives her body a small amount of information about what is going to happen.
She can anticipate the movement instead of experiencing it unexpectedly.
Research on anticipatory vibrotactile cues has also shown that these kinds of cues can help reduce motion sickness.
So the tactile interaction is not only a visual concept. It is based on existing research and experimental results.
⸻
9. Adaptive Seat
The seat is another important part of our system.
Traditional adaptive seats mainly respond to the passenger’s chosen position.
Our concept changes this relationship.
The vehicle predicts movement first.
Then it predicts how the passenger’s body may respond.
The seat prepares the passenger before the movement happens.
For example, before a sharp turn, the side bolster can slightly increase lateral support.
The adjustment is small and happens before the turn.
So the sequence is:
Vehicle predicts movement → body response is predicted → seat prepares → vehicle turns.
⸻
10. Vehicle-Level and Seat-Level Adaptation
We separate the system into vehicle-level and seat-level responses.
Seat-level intervention is personalized.
For example, Alina can receive stronger lateral support while another passenger does not.
Vehicle-level intervention affects everyone, so we only use it when it is necessary.
This creates a simple principle:
Personal problem → seat-level solution first.
Shared vehicle risk → vehicle-level adaptation.
This helps prevent one passenger’s needs from unnecessarily affecting everyone else.
⸻
11. Passenger Conflict
This becomes important when different passengers have different sensitivities.
For example, our third persona is Leo, a 25-year-old passenger who is not sensitive to motion sickness.
Leo prefers a faster and more dynamic ride.
At the same time, Alina needs smoother movement because she is sensitive to motion sickness.
Instead of changing the whole vehicle immediately, the system first gives Alina personalized seat support and tactile cues.
Leo’s seat can remain unchanged.
Only if Alina’s risk becomes very high would the system consider a limited vehicle-level adjustment.
Our priority is:
Safety → Individual intervention → Shared vehicle adaptation.
⸻
12. Persona — Emma
Our second additional persona is Emma, a 9-year-old elementary school student.
Emma often experiences motion sickness, but she has difficulty recognizing or explaining her symptoms.
She may simply become quiet, uncomfortable, or distracted.
The system can detect changes in her gaze, head movement, posture, and the upcoming vehicle movement.
Because she is a child, the intervention should be gentle.
Instead of asking her to understand a complicated interface, the system can automatically provide subtle seat support and tactile feedback.
This shows that our concept can support passengers who cannot clearly communicate their discomfort.
⸻
13. Passenger Recognition
For personalized intervention, the vehicle needs to know who is sitting in the seat.
Passenger recognition could use a smartphone, NFC, a personal vehicle account, or another optional identification method.
When the passenger enters the vehicle, their personal motion profile can be loaded.
The system then combines this profile with the passenger’s current state.
So the vehicle does not simply ask, “Is this road difficult?”
It asks:
“Is this movement difficult for this particular passenger in this particular situation?”
⸻
14. Privacy
However, passenger monitoring creates a privacy concern.
Our system may use cameras to track eye gaze, head movement, and posture.
This means privacy needs to be considered from the beginning.
Possible solutions include local processing, collecting only the necessary information, being transparent about what is detected, and allowing passengers to control or disable certain functions.
This remains an open design consideration for our concept.
⸻
15. Research
Our research question was:
How can a vehicle anticipate and reduce motion sickness before passengers begin to feel uncomfortable?
Our research found three important points.
First, motion sickness can be caused by a mismatch between visual and vestibular information.
Second, research has shown that anticipatory vibrotactile cues before vehicle movements can reduce motion sickness.
Third, recent studies have demonstrated that vibration actuators can be integrated into car seats and used on real roads.
This supports our idea of using the seat itself as an interaction surface.
⸻
16. Storyboard — Alina
Now I will explain our main storyboard.
First, Alina starts working on her presentation during the ride.
Second, the vehicle approaches a winding road with repeated turns.
Third, the system detects that Alina is looking at her screen while upcoming turns increase her motion-sickness risk.
Fourth, the seat prepares her body with lateral support and gives a subtle tactile cue before the turn.
Fifth, Alina anticipates the movement and continues working.
Finally, she arrives with her presentation finished without having to stop working because of motion sickness.
The important change is that the system acts before the discomfort becomes severe.
⸻
17. Overall Interaction
The overall interaction can be summarized in four steps:
Sense → Predict → Prepare → Adapt.
The vehicle senses both movement and passenger state.
It predicts the upcoming movement and the passenger’s possible response.
The seat and vehicle prepare for that movement.
Then the system adapts the experience according to the passenger’s needs.
⸻
18. Final Concept
Our concept is not simply about making the vehicle move more smoothly.
It is about changing the relationship between the vehicle and the human body.
Traditional mobility can be described as:
Road-Aware Mobility.
The vehicle understands the road and adjusts itself.
Our concept moves toward:
Human-Aware Mobility.
The vehicle understands not only the road, but also how different passengers experience that movement.
So our final concept is:
From Road-Aware Mobility to Human-Aware Mobility.
The vehicle does not simply move the passenger.
It understands the passenger and prepares them for the movement.

## C9. Imagine 1 Project Plan (EN)
28 sentyabr · mənbə: TeamA_Imagine1_Project_Plan.txt
DESIGN NARRATIVE · CONTEXTUAL EXPERIENCE DESIGN STUDIO · IMAGINE 1
Contextual Mobility UX
Project Plan — Three Concept Directions · Team A: Mobility & Context
Opening
In our first presentation we mostly reported what already exists. This time we started from a question.
Looking back at our three field observations — the sliding door, the rear-seat screen, and the seats turning
toward each other — we found they all describe the same situation: people sitting in the same vehicle
need different things at the same moment. One passenger needs to get out quickly, another doesn't. One
wants a movie, another wants silence. One can read a phone, another starts to feel sick.
Today's vehicles are designed around one person — the driver — and one fixed experience. Everyone else
has to adapt.
Shared Research Question
How might a vehicle understand each passenger as an individual — who they are,
how their body experiences movement, and how they want to be with others —
instead of offering one fixed experience to everyone inside?
Three directions, three parts of one question
Direction The vehicle understands…
01 Passenger-Centered Personalization Who is sitting here
02 Adaptive Motion Comfort How their body experiences movement
03 Modular Social Pods How they want to be with others
Contextual Mobility UX · Team A · Imagine 1 Project Plan 1
DIRECTION 01
Passenger-Centered Personalization
“The car knows who is driving — but not who is sitting here.”
BACKGROUND Biometric personalization already ships in cars (Hyundai Santa Fe fingerprint, Genesis
GV60 biometric authentication), but only for the driver. Concepts such as Audi
activesphere switch context at the whole-vehicle level — never for the individual
passenger.
THEORY Dourish (2004), What We Talk About When We Talk About Context: context is
relational, not fixed data. “One seat = one setting” is the wrong model — the same
person needs different things in the front seat in the morning and the back seat on a
long trip.
CONCEPT A vehicle that recognizes every passenger and adapts to the combination of person +
seat + moment.
TARGET / CONTEXT Regular users (family car): fingerprint, saved profiles.
Irregular users (taxi, shared car): NFC tap from a phone, temporary profile, no app
download.
KEY INTERACTION Passenger sits down and taps → seat, climate, lighting and content adjust to that
person in that seat.
DIFFERENCE Personalization moves from the driver and the whole vehicle down to each individual
passenger.
OPEN QUESTIONS 1. Why fingerprint / NFC instead of preferences in a ride-hailing app?
2. How are children's biometric data handled — or is the scenario adults only?
3. What does a first-time passenger with no history see (cold start)?
Contextual Mobility UX · Team A · Imagine 1 Project Plan 2
DIRECTION 02
Adaptive Motion Comfort
“From road-aware mobility to human-aware mobility.”
BACKGROUND As driving becomes autonomous, passengers read, work and watch screens instead of
the road — but the car still accelerates, brakes and turns. When the eyes see a stable
screen and the inner ear feels movement, the mismatch causes motion sickness. Less
road-watching means more mismatch.
THEORY Sensory conflict theory (Reason & Brand, 1975): motion sickness comes from a
visual–vestibular mismatch. Diels & Bos (2016), Self-driving carsickness: autonomous
vehicles will make this more common, and design must respond.
CONCEPT A vehicle that estimates each passenger's motion-sickness risk and prepares their
body before the movement happens.
TARGET / CONTEXT Passengers using screens, reading or working — especially motion-sensitive people —
on winding roads and in stop-and-go traffic.
KEY INTERACTION Four signals — vehicle movement, eye gaze, head movement, posture — estimate risk
(not a diagnosis). Before a turn: the seat gently increases side support, a subtle light or
tactile cue signals the direction, and the driving style becomes smoother.
Sense → Predict → Prepare → Adapt
DIFFERENCE Active suspension reacts to the road. This responds to how the person experiences
the road. Seat actuators already exist — what is new is linking predicted vehicle
movement to the passenger's body.
OPEN QUESTIONS 1. Is an interior camera watching the passenger acceptable for privacy?
2. Do light / tactile cues reduce discomfort, or add more stimulation?
3. How do we test without a real car — Wizard of Oz with a chair and video?
Contextual Mobility UX · Team A · Imagine 1 Project Plan 3
DIRECTION 03
Modular Social Pods
“Together and apart as two states of the same vehicle.”
BACKGROUND Flexible-cabin concepts sit at two extremes. Audi urbansphere keeps everyone in one
cabin and softens conflict with swivel seats and privacy screens. Audi Capsule gives
full privacy — but only ever for one person. Neither serves a group that needs to be
together one moment and apart the next.
THEORY Ballendat, Marquardt & Greenberg (2010), Proxemic Interaction: social distance shapes
system behavior — here the passenger sets it physically.
Hornecker & Buur (2006), Getting a Grip on Tangible Interaction: physical space itself
shapes social interaction.
Watts & Urry (2008), Moving Methods, Travelling Times: travel time is an opportunity,
not wasted time.
CONCEPT A vehicle of independent pods that join into one group cabin (shared screen, sound,
conversation) or separate into private pods. Passengers choose — not the vehicle.
TARGET / CONTEXT Families, friends and coworkers on long trips, where one person — introverted or
overstimulated — needs to step out of the group experience without ending the trip for
everyone.
KEY INTERACTION Pods connect → shared screen and audio turn on. A pod detaches → that space
becomes quiet and private.
DIFFERENCE Does not choose between social and solo at the design stage — both are states
switchable in the moment. Audi × Italdesign autonomous docking tests give a technical
precedent.
OPEN QUESTIONS 1. Does separation happen while driving or only at stops? (safety)
2. Can the feeling of separation be created without physically splitting the vehicle —
through sound zones, light and screens?
Contextual Mobility UX · Team A · Imagine 1 Project Plan 4
SYNTHESIS
How the Three Directions Connect
All three directions share one design principle:
The vehicle adapts to each person — instead of each person adapting to the
vehicle.
The directions also overlap. Personalized motion comfort (02) needs to know who the passenger is —
exactly what 01 provides. The solo mode of 03 could give a passenger feeling motion sickness a calm,
private space.
Selection criteria for Imagine 2
Connection to context · Originality of the experience · Interaction potential · Development potential
Plan for next week
Section Deliverable
Research Foundation (1–5) Full version of each section for the chosen direction, with sources
Scenario (7) 4–6 panel scenario sketch for each direction
Sketch Exploration (8) 15–20 variations of form, space, UI and touchpoints
System Diagram (9) Input → Interpretation → Response → Feedback for each direction
First Prototype (10) Sketch + AI visualization
Contextual Mobility UX · Team A · Imagine 1 Project Plan 5

means something different depending on who sits in it.
Shared space, single-user design More features, more work Those who need care have the

How can a vehicle anticipate and reduce motion

sickness before passengers begin to feel
Uses autonomous rides between university and home to work on
uncomfortable? presentations. Sensitive to motion sickness, especially when looking at a

When the eyes see a stable screen but the body feels turning and braking, the

A new driver who can't ask for quiet
In about 40% of teen-driver crashes with passengers,
Yuna, 19 — licensed 6 months
the driver was looking at or talking to a passenger in
Introverted, easily distracted by conversation, music and sudden noise.

University of Iowa / AAA Foundation — ~1,700 crash videos of drivers aged 16–19.
Critical moment More passengers, more risk: 54% of crashes with one passenger, 74% with two
or more.
On a family road trip, the car enters a busy highway. She needs full focus to
merge, while her siblings keep talking and laughing. She tries to drive
Why today's solutions fail
through the distraction instead.
Limousine and taxi partitions are all-or-nothing, built for privacy from strangers,
and leave the front passenger unblocked. Fully autonomous cars aren't a near-
term answer.

## C13. Presentation Script (EN), Imagine 2
5 oktyabr · mənbə: TeamA_Presentation_Script_EN.txt
Contextual Mobility UX — Presentation
Script (Imagine 2)
Team A · Mobility & Context · October 5, 2026
How to use this script
This script goes with the full 34-slide deck (TeamA_Project_Proposal_Imagine2.pdf) and gives
what to say on each slide, in order. Estimated time: 15–18 minutes.
Part Slides Speaker Time
Opening 1–3 Sunny (team leader) 2 min
Direction 01 — Passenger recognition 4–20 Sunny 7 min
Direction 02 — Motion comfort 21–27 Author of the motion comfort idea 3–4 min
Direction 03 — Capsule group travel 28–31 Author of the capsule idea 2–3 min
Synthesis and closing 32–34 Sunny 2 min
Notes in italics are reminders, not lines to read. Don't read the slides aloud: the script explains
each slide rather than repeating it.
Opening (slides 1–3)
Slide 1 — Title
Hello everyone. We are Team A, and our topic was Mobility & Context. Today we're presenting
the results of the Imagine stage for our Contextual Mobility UX project: three different design
directions, and one shared question that connects them.
Slide 2 — Where we started
First, we want to be honest. In our first presentation, we mostly listed what already exists, and
our three parts felt disconnected. This time we started from a question, not from facts.
We looked again at our three observations from Hyundai UX Studio Seoul: the sliding door, the
rear-seat screen, and the seats that turn toward each other. We realized all three describe the
same situation: people in the same vehicle need different things at the same moment.
One passenger needs to get out quickly, another doesn't. One wants a movie, another wants
silence. One can read a phone, another starts to feel sick. The driver needs to focus, while the
family keeps talking. Yet today's cars are designed around one person — the driver — and one
fixed experience. Everyone else has to adapt.
Team A · Presentation Script · 1/10
Slide 3 — Shared research question
So our shared question is: how might a vehicle adapt to each person inside it, instead of
asking every person to adapt to the vehicle?
We answered this question from three angles. The first direction is about the car knowing who
is sitting where. The second is about understanding how each body experiences movement.
The third is about adapting to how people want to be with each other. We'll show each one,
and at the end, how they connect.
Direction 01 — Research (slides 4–9)
Slide 4 — Passenger-Centered Personalization
Our first direction: a car that knows who is sitting where, and lets each passenger's experience
follow them.
Slide 5 — How our question changed
At the start, our question was simple: should personalization be tied to the seat, the person, or
the combination of person + seat + moment?
While working on our personas, the answer appeared: it depends on the passenger. For an
adult, personalization is tied to the person and moves with them. For a baby, it's tied to the
child seat and the guardian. For a guest, it's temporary and erased when they leave. Two new
topics also came up: permission and conflict.
So we expanded our question: how might a shared car recognize who is sitting where, let each
passenger's experience follow the person rather than the seat, protect those who can't control
it themselves, and fairly share what must be shared? In short: how might we design a car where
comfort belongs to people, not seats?
Slide 6 — Background
The technology already exists — but none of it knows who is sitting where.
Socially, cars are shared: parents, adult children, babies and guests, often in the same seat on
different days. But design is still built for the driver. Bose, for example, optimized its headrest
audio for the driver's seat, because it's the only position in the cabin occupied 100% of the
time.
Culturally, personalization is becoming an expectation. In DTS's 2022 survey, 69% of drivers
said advanced personalization is important, described as the car "knowing you and your
occupants." Services like Uber Comfort and Waymo already remember rider preferences.
The table shows what each existing solution can't do: Hyundai and Genesis fingerprint systems
recognize only the driver; memory seats have existed since 1957 but remember a slot, not a
person; Hyundai's Separated Sound Zone can give each seat its own sound, but doesn't know
whose sound belongs where.
Team A · Presentation Script · 2/10
Slide 7 — Key findings
We have three key findings.
First, cars remember seats, not people. Memory seats work with buttons and are usually only
in the front. Rear-seat memory exists only in top luxury models like the S-Class. We see this in
our own lives too: my brother always reclines the front seat in my father's car. Every time I get
in after him, I have to readjust it, because his change stays in the seat.
Second, personal technologies either serve the driver or work alone. The pieces exist, but
nothing connects them.
Third, needs conflict, and some passengers can't control their own. Climate zones share the
same cabin air, so the difference between zones stays small. Babies, children and guests can't
adjust settings themselves. And the only "permission" system today is the child lock — which
locks everything or nothing.
Slide 8 — Insight / tension
We summarized all of this in one sentence: cars are personalized for one person, but used by
many.
As Dourish argued in 2004, context is not fixed data — it's created by who is interacting and
how. Today's cars, however, treat a seat as a fixed setting.
This creates three contradictions. A shared space is designed for a single user. More
personalization means more work, because every new feature is another menu, and none of
them knows who it's serving. And those who need the most care — the baby and the guest —
have the least control.
Slide 9 — Design opportunity
Our opportunity is not to invent new seat technology. It's to design the layer that connects
"who" with "where."
We have three design moves. First, the experience follows the person: one touch on the seat
screen brings your settings to whichever seat you sit in, and when you get up, the seat closes
your profile and returns to neutral. Second, we protect those who can't control it themselves:
guardian-linked profiles, permission per zone, and a simple guest mode for anyone not
recognized. Third, we share what must be shared, fairly.
In daily life, the system should be invisible, coming forward only at the moments that need it. In
the future, the same layer could work for car sharing and robotaxis.
Team A · Presentation Script · 3/10
Direction 01 — Design proposal (slides 10–20)
Slide 10 — Personas
We didn't choose diverse users — we chose extreme users. The idea is simple: if the system
works for a baby who can't control anything, and for a guest who has no connection to the car,
it works for everyone in between.
Mina, 8 months. She has just fallen asleep. Her 7-year-old brother Noah, sitting next to her,
turns the A/C to the coldest setting and turns up his cartoon. Mina wakes up crying. Her mother
Emily is driving: she can't see the baby and can't turn around to fix anything. In our system,
Noah's sound and cold air stay in his own zone, and only Emily's fingerprint can change Mina's
zone.
Alex, 21. His father drives him to university every morning. On the way, they stop on a narrow
street to pick up his younger brother Ethan. Ethan can only get in from the right door, so Alex
switches seats in a few seconds — and his music stays in the old seat. In our system, the old
seat closes his profile, and one touch moves his settings and music to the new seat.
Margaret, 72, the grandmother. She visits once or twice a month. She feels cold, but doesn't
understand the menus and doesn't want to distract her son driving at night, so she stays silent.
In our system, an unrecognized fingerprint opens a simple guest screen. Local heating warms
only her, and her settings are erased when she leaves.
Slide 11 — Conflict rules
One of the key questions in the professor's feedback was: when there's a conflict, who gets
priority?
First, we divided resources into two groups. Seat position, massage, seat heating and cooling,
radiant panels, personal sound zones and the seat screen are personal and don't create
conflict. Conflict only remains in the shared cabin air and the main speakers. Our principle:
move as many services as possible into personal resources.
The priority order is: safety, meaning the driver's navigation and alerts; then the vulnerable
passenger, such as a baby; then equal passengers.
For temperature, we found an interesting rule: local heating is easy, but local cooling is hard. So
the shared air is kept on the cool side, and anyone who wants warmth is warmed locally. If two
equal adults still disagree, the system asks both: "Meet in the middle?" Permission is simple:
everyone controls their own zone, and only a guardian can change a baby's zone.
Slide 12 — Sketch exploration: sensor and placement
Our first question was: where and how do you touch? We explored three forms of fingerprint
sensor. A tunnel-shaped recess can be found without looking, so it's best for the driver. A round
recess suits the seat screen and feels familiar, like a phone. A raised form is easy to feel, but
can be pressed by accident.
Team A · Presentation Script · 4/10
Then we explored locations: the door handle, the armrest and the seatbelt. The seatbelt needs
no extra action, but doesn't work for a child seat. Our hand sketches and their AI visualizations
are shown side by side.
Slide 13 — UI states
We sketched seven states of one seat screen: waiting, recognized, moving with you, guardian
selection, protected zone, guest mode and the conflict question. These are sketches, not a
finished interface. What matters is what the system says and what it asks of the passenger in
each state.
Slide 14 — Space, light and service
This slide shows the system at the scale of the whole cabin: who sits where; Noah's music and
cool air next to Mina's quiet, warm zone; the light moving with a person when they change
seats; a light ring that shows the screen's state; and profiles created once, at home, on a
phone. In total, we made 18 sketch explorations.
Slide 15 — Storyboard: Alex
Alex gets in in the morning and touches the screen: "Welcome back, Alex!" His father stops to
pick up Ethan. Alex moves seats, and his old seat closes his profile. With one touch, his music
continues in the new seat. Ethan gets his own settings. And his father keeps driving without
touching anything.
Slide 16 — The same story from above
We show the same scenario from above, because that's where the core of the idea is clearest:
the light and the music move with the person, and the old seat is left empty.
Slide 17 — Mina and Margaret scenarios
In Mina's scenario, Emily selects the baby's zone once. When Noah touches Mina's screen, it
says "This is Mina's zone," and nothing changes. While driving, Emily touches the sensor next
to the window switches and asks: "How is Mina?" The system answers by voice: "Mina is
asleep. Her zone is quiet and at 24 degrees." Emily keeps her eyes on the road.
In Margaret's scenario, she presses the big "Warmer" button. Her seat and footwell warm up,
while the shared air doesn't change. When she leaves, the seat returns to neutral.
Slide 18 — System diagram
At the top is the system structure: the user touches and speaks through the interface; the AI
system knows who sits where, decides on permissions and conflicts, and controls the mobility
— the seat, the sound zones and the climate. At the bottom is the loop every event follows:
Input, Interpretation, Response, Feedback. Each time, the system asks five questions: who,
where, what, is it allowed, and is there a conflict?
Team A · Presentation Script · 5/10
Slide 19 — Seat states
When a seat is empty, it's in a neutral position. When someone sits down, the weight sensor
detects it and the screen asks for a fingerprint. If the person is recognized, their personal
profile opens. If not, guest mode. If a child seat is installed and a guardian selects it, the
protected profile. When the seat stays empty for a few seconds, the profile closes and the
music stops. This also protects privacy: whoever sits there after you sees nothing of yours.
Slide 20 — Open questions and next prototype
There are still questions we need to answer. The most important: why a fingerprint, not an app?
Our draft answer: not everyone has a phone — a baby, a child, a grandmother. A phone knows
who you are, but not where you sit. And a fingerprint permission can't be borrowed like a
parent's phone. In short: the app prepares, the fingerprint recognizes.
Other questions: can the driver find the sensor without looking? How do other caregivers, like a
grandparent, get permission? How many seconds before a profile closes? And what happens in
cars without cooled seats?
At this stage, our outcome is sketches and AI visualizations. The most realistic first piece to
build is the fingerprint seat screen and its states. We'll decide the final scope after your
feedback.
Direction 02 — Motion Comfort While Driving (slides 21–27)
Slide 21 — Title
Our second direction is a move from road-aware mobility to human-aware mobility: the vehicle
prepares the passenger's body before the movement happens.
Slide 22 — Problem and persona
As vehicles become more autonomous, passengers work, read and watch videos during the
ride. But the car still accelerates, brakes and turns. The eyes see a stable screen while the
body feels the turns. This sensory mismatch causes nausea, dizziness and headaches. The
problem is that people usually notice it only after it has started.
Our question: how can a vehicle anticipate and reduce motion sickness before passengers
begin to feel uncomfortable?
Our persona is Alina, a 20-year-old design student. She uses her rides between university and
home to work on her presentations. Critical moment: she has to finish her slides on the way, but
on a winding road she starts to feel sick. She has to close her tablet, look out the window and
wait — or take medicine. The ride stops being useful time.
Slide 23 — Key findings
We have three findings. First, motion sickness comes from a mismatch between vision and the
balance system, especially when looking at a screen. Second, vibration cues given before a
Team A · Presentation Script · 6/10
movement help: in Reuten and colleagues' 2024 study, symptoms dropped by about 40% on a
test track, and Kehl and colleagues saw the same effect on public roads. Third, these cues can
be delivered through the seat: actuators hidden in the seat gave a signal about 0.7 seconds
before turns, braking and acceleration, and passengers didn't need to look away from their
screen.
Today, the passenger reacts after the discomfort starts. In our proposal, the system sees the
risk in advance, intervenes as little as possible, and Alina keeps working.
To be honest: the evidence is strongest in real-road studies, and some lab studies showed
smaller effects. The effect of lateral seat support is still a hypothesis and needs to be tested in
the prototype.
Slide 24 — System logic
The system combines three kinds of information: the vehicle's movement — speed, braking,
steering and upcoming turns; the passenger's state — gaze, head movement, posture and seat
pressure; and a personal profile — previous sensitivity. The system doesn't diagnose; it only
estimates the risk.
If the risk is low, it does nothing. If it's medium, it gives slight side support and a small vibration
before the turn. If it's high, stronger support, earlier cues and smoother driving. The main
principle: minimum necessary intervention. For example, if a left turn is coming, the left side
of the seat vibrates gently. Alina can turn it off or change its intensity.
Slide 25 — Personalization and conflict
Different passengers get different responses. Alina is sensitive and gets support. Emma is 9;
she often gets carsick but can't explain it. The system notices changes in her gaze, head and
posture and gives gentle support automatically. Leo is 25, not sensitive, and likes a dynamic
ride — his seat doesn't change.
The conflict rule: a personal problem is solved at the seat level first. Only if Alina's risk becomes
very high is there a limited change at the vehicle level. The order is: safety, individual
intervention, shared adaptation.
The open risk is privacy: cameras track gaze, head and posture. Data must be processed
locally, kept to a minimum, communicated openly to the passenger, and under their control.
Slide 26 — Sketch exploration
On the left are the hand sketches: 13 groups and more than 30 quick variations, from where the
vibration is felt, to support levels, to whether the risk indicator is hidden or visible. On the right
is the AI visualization of the same explorations.
Slide 27 — Storyboard: Alina
Alina starts working on her presentation. A winding road is ahead. Her gaze on the screen and
the turns raise the risk. The seat strengthens its side support and gives a vibration before the
Team A · Presentation Script · 7/10
turn. Alina feels the movement in advance and keeps working. In the end, she arrives with her
slides finished. The key difference: the system acts before the discomfort becomes severe.
Direction 03 — Introvert-Friendly Group Travel (slides 28–31)
Slide 28 — Title
Our third direction is one vehicle made of four independent capsules: travel together, split up,
or step back from the group without leaving the ride.
Slide 29 — Persona and evidence
Our persona is Yuna, 19, who has had her driver's license for six months. She's introverted and
easily distracted by conversation, music and sudden noise. She cares a lot about driving safely,
but she doesn't want to ask her family to be quiet, because she's afraid it will look like she
"can't handle it."
Critical moment: on a family road trip, the car enters a busy highway. Yuna needs full
concentration to merge, while her siblings in the back keep talking and laughing. Instead of
asking them to stop, she tries to drive through the distraction.
There is evidence that this is a real risk. The University of Iowa and the AAA Foundation
analyzed about 1,700 crash videos of drivers aged 16–19: in about 40% of crashes with
passengers, the driver was looking at or talking to a passenger in the six seconds before the
crash. The risk also grows with the number of passengers: 54% of crashes involved one
passenger, 74% involved two or more.
Today's solutions aren't enough. Limousine and taxi partitions are either fully open or fully
closed, are designed for privacy from strangers, and don't separate the front passenger at all.
And fully autonomous cars aren't an answer for the near future.
Slide 30 — Concept and decision logic
There are four single-seat capsules and three ways to travel. Together: the capsules connect
into one car, and the front capsule drives. Split: if people are going to different places, the
capsules separate before the trip. Every capsule has a driver mode, so no autonomous driving
is needed. Close off: during the ride, any capsule's window can close. The driver can separate
acoustically to focus, or a passenger to rest.
Decision logic: only the person inside controls their window. The driver's closed window takes
priority, and the others keep talking. On a highway, the system only suggests closing it, and
Yuna confirms with one tap.
Safety: a closed window doesn't isolate completely. Sensors detect urgent sounds — distress,
crying and alarms — and pass them into the closed capsule. Light indicators show who is
separated.
Team A · Presentation Script · 8/10
Slide 31 — Sketches, scenario and open risks
On the left is the hand sketch, in the middle the AI visualization. The scenario: a family trip,
capsules connected, everyone chatting. As the highway approaches, the dashboard suggests
that Yuna close her window. She taps the button on the steering wheel. Her window closes, and
a light shows the group. Yuna merges calmly and opens her window again after the highway.
Open risks: crash safety and emergency exit with closed windows; splitting only works if each
passenger has a driver's license; quiet but urgent signals; whether a closed window makes
others feel left out; and changing destinations mid-route.
Synthesis and closing (slides 32–34)
Slide 32 — Three directions, one principle
We showed three different ideas, but all of them rest on the same principle: the vehicle adapts
to each person, instead of each person adapting to the vehicle.
The first direction adapts to identity, the second to the body, and the third to social needs. The
ideas also complement each other. The recognition system from Direction 01 could load Alina's
motion profile in Direction 02. Direction 02 uses the same logic as Direction 01's conflict rule:
solve it at the seat level first. And Direction 03 builds on the same idea of personal zones as the
sound zones in Direction 01.
Slide 33 — Comparison
We compared the three directions using the four criteria from the syllabus.
In terms of connection to context, Direction 01 is grounded in everyday family life and our own
observations, Direction 02 in strong research evidence, and Direction 03 in crash data. In terms
of originality, Direction 01 offers a layer that decides who sits where and permission per zone;
Direction 02 offers the seat as a predictive interface for the body; Direction 03 offers a vehicle
that physically joins, splits and closes off. In terms of interaction, Direction 01 uses touch, light,
voice and sound zones; Direction 02 uses vibration and seat movement; Direction 03 uses
windows, buttons and light indicators. In terms of development potential, Direction 01's seat
screen can be built, but its scope needs to narrow; Direction 02 can be tested with a seat and
vibration using a Wizard of Oz method; and Direction 03 still has open questions about physical
safety and licensing.
We'll decide which direction to develop, and our plan for next week, based on your feedback
today.
Slide 34 — Thank you
Thank you for listening. We look forward to your questions and feedback.
Note: don't announce a winner in the comparison. The choice should be made after the
professor's feedback.
Team A · Presentation Script · 9/10
Likely professor questions and prepared answers
