## C14. Why Better Than Manual (KR docx)
oktyabr · mənbə: Why_Better_Than_Manual_KR.txt
“왜 수동 조절보다 나은가?”
Direction 01 — 탑승자 중심 개인화 · 현재 가설과 리서치 방법
짧은 답: 부분적으로 알고 있다. 논리적인 근거는 있지만, 이를 뒷받침하는 증거는 아직 없다. 교수님은 “입증하라”고 하셨다.
현재 가설 (증거 없음)
이 가설들을 실제 데이터로 검증해야 한다.
이번 주에 할 수 있는 리서치
1. 짧은 인터뷰 (가장 빠르고 가장 강력한 방법)
각 페르소나별로 2–3명과 10분 정도 대화하는 것으로 충분하다.
부모: “운전 중에 뒷좌석 아이를 위해 무언가를 조절해야 했던 적이 있나요? 어떻게 하셨나요? 그때 어떤 기분이었나요?”
10대 / 대학생: “가족 차에서 좌석이나 음악을 매번 바꾸나요? 바꾸지 않는다면, 왜인가요?”
택시 이용자: “택시에서 온도나 소리가 마음에 들지 않을 때 어떻게 하나요?”
가장 가치 있는 답변은 “아무것도 안 하고 그냥 참아요” 같은 답변이다. 이는 수동 조절이 작동하지 않는다는 직접적인 증거가 된다.
2. 관찰
가족 차량으로 이동할 때 누가, 언제, 무엇을 조절하는지 기록한다. 조절하고 싶었지만 하지 않은 사람은 누구인가? 누가 누구에게 부탁했는가? 운전자의 주의가 도로에서 벗어났는가?
3. 기존 제품에서 얻는 간접 증거
자동차 제조사들은 사람들이 매번 다시 조절하는 것을 번거로워하기 때문에 메모리 시트(memory seat) 기능을 만들었다. 하지만 이 기능은 보통 운전석, 때로는 조수석에만 적용된다. 즉, 문제는 이미 인식되었지만 뒷좌석 탑승자에게는 해결되지 않았다. Key Findings에 활용하기 좋은 사실이다.
4. 온라인 불만과 리뷰
자동차 커뮤니티나 리뷰에서 “뒷좌석 공조”, “rear seat climate control”, “택시 온도” 같은 주제로 사람들의 불만을 찾아본다. 실제 사람들의 말은 시나리오를 더 설득력 있게 만든다.
발표에 활용하기
인터뷰에서 실제 인용 1–2개, 관찰에서 구체적인 순간 하나를 찾으면, “왜 수동 조절보다 나은가?” 부분이 가설에서 증거로 바뀐다. 교수님이 원하시는 것이 바로 이것이다.

## C15. Team A Briefing: Direction 01 & 02
9 oktyabr · mənbə: TeamA_Briefing_Direction01_02.txt
Team A — Briefing for Tomorrow's Idea
Session
Contextual Mobility UX · Design Narrative · prepared October 9, 2026
1. What the professor asked for
His feedback (translated):
Regarding the passenger identification method using a fingerprint sensor, I question
whether fingerprint recognition is really such a necessary security technology. It may
also be more costly to introduce and implement than ordinary camera sensors or visual
recognition methods. Since the essence of this integrated course is designing spatial
experiences based on visuals, video and interaction, I'd like you to think about an
appropriate solution that achieves maximum effect at low cost, using existing
infrastructure such as camera sensors and an interface experience layer, rather than
focusing on physical devices or expensive hardware.
Before the next class, please choose one of the three concept directions and develop its
scenario further. Analyze from multiple angles what visual and video interactions and
spatial experiences three different personas each have within one and the same scenario
situation, and what differentiated value is created through this.
I also expect you to look back at the detailed points we discussed in the previous Google
Chat feedback and develop the content with greater depth.
What this means for us:
Choose one of our three directions. Not a brand-new idea.
Visual, video and interaction first. The result should be an experience you can see, not a
mechanism.
Low cost, existing infrastructure. Use what cars already have:
interior camera (already used for driver and occupant monitoring)
ambient lighting (color-changing, zoned)
screens (center display, rear screens, the passenger's own phone or tablet)
speakers, A/C, seat heating
Avoid expensive hardware: fingerprint sensors, smart-glass windows, separating
capsules, seat vibration or movement mechanisms.
One scenario, three personas: the same situation, and each persona experiences different
visual interactions and spatial experiences.
Differentiated value: what each persona gains, analyzed from several angles.
Deadline: next class, Monday, October 12. Presentation limit: about 10 minutes.
Team A · Briefing · 1/5
2. Direction 01 — Passenger-Centered Personalization
Core idea: a car that knows who is sitting where, and lets each passenger's experience follow
them.
Problem we found:
Cars are personalized for one person (the driver), but used by many.
Memory seats remember a seat slot (buttons 1–2–3), not a person. Rear-seat memory
exists only in luxury models.
When someone changes seats, their settings stay in the old seat. Example from our own
life: a brother always reclines the front seat, and the next person has to readjust it every
time.
Passengers who can't control anything themselves (babies, children, elderly guests) have
the least control.
The only "permission" system today is the child lock, which is all-or-nothing.
Personas (one family):
Persona Situation Need
Mina, 8 months (with Mina is asleep. Noah turns the A/C cold A protected zone that only a
mother Emily, brother and his cartoon loud. Emily is driving parent can change
Noah 7) and can't see or help.
Alex, 21 (father Daniel, Dad stops on a narrow street to pick up His experience should move
brother Ethan 10) Ethan. Alex quickly moves seats, and with him
his music stays in the old seat.
Margaret, 72 She feels cold, doesn't understand the A simple guest experience,
(grandmother, visiting) menus, and doesn't want to distract her warmed without changing
son driving at night. everyone's air
System logic we already designed (still valid):
Each seat is a personal zone (sound, light, temperature, screen).
When someone leaves a seat, the profile closes and the seat returns to neutral (privacy).
Priority in conflicts: safety → vulnerable passenger (baby) → equal passengers.
Temperature: solve locally first (seat heating), shared air stays cool, ask both before
compromising ("Meet in the middle?").
What must change after feedback:
Fingerprint → camera. The interior camera recognizes who sits where. No touch needed.
From system logic to visible experience. Ideas we already have:
Light as a personal signature: each person gets their own color of light around their
seat.
Light follows the person: when Alex moves seats, his color flows through the cabin to
his new seat.
Team A · Briefing · 2/5
Visual quiet zone: Mina's zone dims into a soft "night corner" that everyone can see.
Visual guidance for guests: Margaret controls things through large, simple light
signals instead of menus.
Visual negotiation: when two people want different temperatures, two colors move
toward each other on the ceiling and meet in the middle.
Open questions: privacy of camera recognition (process inside the car, allow turning it off,
don't store guests' faces); how lights are distinguishable without being distracting for the
driver.
3. Direction 02 — Motion Comfort While Driving
Core idea: from road-aware to human-aware mobility. The vehicle prepares the passenger for
movement before it happens.
Problem we found:
As cars become more autonomous, passengers work, read and watch videos during the
ride.
Looking at a stable screen while the body feels turns and braking causes motion sickness
(a mismatch between vision and the balance system).
People usually notice it only after it starts, then they close the tablet, look outside, wait, or
take medicine.
Personas (already in one shared ride):
Persona Situation Need
Alina, 20 (design Must finish her slides on the ride. On a Keep working without getting
student) winding road, she starts to feel sick. sick
Emma, 9 Often carsick but can't explain it. Help without having to
describe her symptoms
Leo, 25 Not sensitive, likes a dynamic ride. Nothing should change for
him
Evidence we have:
Motion sickness comes from visual–vestibular sensory conflict.
Anticipatory cues before turns reduced car sickness by about 40% on a test track (Reuten
et al., 2024) and also worked on public roads (Kehl et al.).
⚠️ These studies used vibration cues. For a visual version, we need new evidence on
visual cues.
System logic we already designed (still valid):
Inputs: vehicle movement (turns, braking), passenger state (gaze, head, posture via
camera), personal sensitivity.
Team A · Briefing · 3/5
Risk levels: low → nothing; medium → subtle cue; high → stronger cue + smoother driving.
Principle: minimum necessary intervention. Seat level first, vehicle level only when needed.
What must change after feedback:
Vibration and seat support → visual cues. Ideas:
Light flowing before a turn: ambient light moves across the cabin in the direction of
the coming turn.
Motion cues on screens: dots or patterns at the edge of the passenger's screen move
with the car. (Apple already does this on iPhone with "Vehicle Motion Cues" in iOS 18,
which shows it's possible with software only.)
Window or ceiling visuals that show the road ahead or the horizon.
How to be different from Apple: cabin-wide and personal — each passenger gets cues in
their own zone, based on their own sensitivity, while Leo's zone stays unchanged. This
connects to the course example of Holoride (vehicle speed, rotation and position creating
real-time visual experiences).
Open questions: evidence for visual cues; privacy of gaze tracking; cues must not distract the
driver.
4. Task for tomorrow
Please bring 2–3 ideas on how our concepts could become a visual or interactive video
experience using only a camera, ambient lighting and screens. Quick sketches, AI images, or a
few sentences are all fine.
For each idea, answer briefly:
1. What do people see? (light, screen, projection, color, motion)
2. When does it happen? (which moment of the ride)
3. Who is it for? (which persona)
4. Why does it matter? (what value it creates for that persona)
Tomorrow we'll look at all the ideas together and choose one direction.
5. Context to paste into AI tools
We are a 3-person team in a university design studio course called "Contextual Mobility
UX." The course focuses on designing spatial experiences in vehicles using visuals, video
and interaction. Our professor asked us to use low-cost existing car infrastructure — an
interior camera, ambient lighting and screens — instead of expensive hardware. We must
choose one of two directions and show one shared scenario where three different
personas each have a different visual and spatial experience.
Team A · Briefing · 4/5
Direction 01: A car that knows who is sitting where (via the interior camera) and makes
each passenger's personal space visible with light and screens. Personas in one family:
Mina (8-month-old baby who needs a quiet, protected zone), Alex (21, whose music and
settings should follow him when he changes seats), Margaret (72, a visiting grandmother
who needs simple visual guidance).
Direction 02: A car that predicts motion sickness and gives passengers visual cues
before turns and braking, using ambient light and screens. Personas in one ride: Alina
(20, working on a tablet, sensitive to motion sickness), Emma (9, gets carsick but can't
explain it), Leo (25, not sensitive, wants nothing to change).
Give me [3] ideas for visual or interactive video experiences for Direction [01/02]. For
each: what people see, when it happens, which persona it's for, and what value it
creates.

## C16. brief.md: sabahkı ideya sessiyası üçün briefing
9 oktyabr · mənbə: 5b5c190b-brief.md
# Team A — Briefing for Tomorrow's Idea Session
Contextual Mobility UX · Design Narrative · prepared October 9, 2026
## 1. What the professor asked for
**His feedback (translated):**
> Regarding the passenger identification method using a fingerprint sensor, I question whether fingerprint recognition is really such a necessary security technology. It may also be more costly to introduce and implement than ordinary camera sensors or visual recognition methods. Since the essence of this integrated course is designing spatial experiences based on visuals, video and interaction, I'd like you to think about an appropriate solution that achieves maximum effect at low cost, using existing infrastructure such as camera sensors and an interface experience layer, rather than focusing on physical devices or expensive hardware.
>
> Before the next class, please choose one of the three concept directions and develop its scenario further. Analyze from multiple angles what visual and video interactions and spatial experiences three different personas each have within one and the same scenario situation, and what differentiated value is created through this.
>
> I also expect you to look back at the detailed points we discussed in the previous Google Chat feedback and develop the content with greater depth.
**What this means for us:**
- **Choose one of our three directions.** Not a brand-new idea.
- **Visual, video and interaction first.** The result should be an experience you can *see*, not a mechanism.
- **Low cost, existing infrastructure.** Use what cars already have:
- interior camera (already used for driver and occupant monitoring)
- ambient lighting (color-changing, zoned)
- screens (center display, rear screens, the passenger's own phone or tablet)
- speakers, A/C, seat heating
- **Avoid expensive hardware:** fingerprint sensors, smart-glass windows, separating capsules, seat vibration or movement mechanisms.
- **One scenario, three personas:** the same situation, and each persona experiences different visual interactions and spatial experiences.
- **Differentiated value:** what each persona gains, analyzed from several angles.
**Deadline:** next class, Monday, October 12. Presentation limit: about 10 minutes.
## 2. Direction 01 — Passenger-Centered Personalization
**Core idea:** a car that knows who is sitting where, and lets each passenger's experience follow them.
**Problem we found:**
- Cars are personalized for one person (the driver), but used by many.
- Memory seats remember a seat slot (buttons 1–2–3), not a person. Rear-seat memory exists only in luxury models.
- When someone changes seats, their settings stay in the old seat. Example from our own life: a brother always reclines the front seat, and the next person has to readjust it every time.
- Passengers who can't control anything themselves (babies, children, elderly guests) have the least control.
- The only "permission" system today is the child lock, which is all-or-nothing.
**Personas (one family):**
| Persona | Situation | Need |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Mina, 8 months** (with mother Emily, brother Noah 7) | Mina is asleep. Noah turns the A/C cold and his cartoon loud. Emily is driving and can't see or help. | A protected zone that only a parent can change |
| **Alex, 21** (father Daniel, brother Ethan 10) | Dad stops on a narrow street to pick up Ethan. Alex quickly moves seats, and his music stays in the old seat. | His experience should move with him |
| **Margaret, 72** (grandmother, visiting) | She feels cold, doesn't understand the menus, and doesn't want to distract her son driving at night. | A simple guest experience, warmed without changing everyone's air |
**System logic we already designed (still valid):**
- Each seat is a personal zone (sound, light, temperature, screen).
- When someone leaves a seat, the profile closes and the seat returns to neutral (privacy).
- Priority in conflicts: safety → vulnerable passenger (baby) → equal passengers.
- Temperature: solve locally first (seat heating), shared air stays cool, ask both before compromising ("Meet in the middle?").
**What must change after feedback:**
- **Fingerprint → camera.** The interior camera recognizes who sits where. No touch needed.
- **From system logic to visible experience.** Ideas we already have:
- **Light as a personal signature:** each person gets their own color of light around their seat.
- **Light follows the person:** when Alex moves seats, his color flows through the cabin to his new seat.
- **Visual quiet zone:** Mina's zone dims into a soft "night corner" that everyone can see.
- **Visual guidance for guests:** Margaret controls things through large, simple light signals instead of menus.
- **Visual negotiation:** when two people want different temperatures, two colors move toward each other on the ceiling and meet in the middle.
**Open questions:** privacy of camera recognition (process inside the car, allow turning it off, don't store guests' faces); how lights are distinguishable without being distracting for the driver.
## 3. Direction 02 — Motion Comfort While Driving
**Core idea:** from road-aware to human-aware mobility. The vehicle prepares the passenger for movement before it happens.
**Problem we found:**
- As cars become more autonomous, passengers work, read and watch videos during the ride.
- Looking at a stable screen while the body feels turns and braking causes motion sickness (a mismatch between vision and the balance system).
- People usually notice it only after it starts, then they close the tablet, look outside, wait, or take medicine.
**Personas (already in one shared ride):**
| Persona | Situation | Need |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Alina, 20** (design student) | Must finish her slides on the ride. On a winding road, she starts to feel sick. | Keep working without getting sick |
| **Emma, 9** | Often carsick but can't explain it. | Help without having to describe her symptoms |
| **Leo, 25** | Not sensitive, likes a dynamic ride. | Nothing should change for him |
**Evidence we have:**
- Motion sickness comes from visual–vestibular sensory conflict.
- Anticipatory cues before turns reduced car sickness by about 40% on a test track (Reuten et al., 2024) and also worked on public roads (Kehl et al.).
- ⚠️ These studies used **vibration** cues. For a visual version, we need new evidence on **visual** cues.
**System logic we already designed (still valid):**
- Inputs: vehicle movement (turns, braking), passenger state (gaze, head, posture via camera), personal sensitivity.
- Risk levels: low → nothing; medium → subtle cue; high → stronger cue + smoother driving.
- Principle: minimum necessary intervention. Seat level first, vehicle level only when needed.
**What must change after feedback:**
- **Vibration and seat support → visual cues.** Ideas:
- **Light flowing before a turn:** ambient light moves across the cabin in the direction of the coming turn.
- **Motion cues on screens:** dots or patterns at the edge of the passenger's screen move with the car. (Apple already does this on iPhone with "Vehicle Motion Cues" in iOS 18, which shows it's possible with software only.)
- **Window or ceiling visuals** that show the road ahead or the horizon.
- **How to be different from Apple:** cabin-wide and personal — each passenger gets cues in their own zone, based on their own sensitivity, while Leo's zone stays unchanged. This connects to the course example of Holoride (vehicle speed, rotation and position creating real-time visual experiences).
**Open questions:** evidence for visual cues; privacy of gaze tracking; cues must not distract the driver.
## 4. Task for tomorrow
Please bring **2–3 ideas** on how our concepts could become a **visual or interactive video experience** using only a camera, ambient lighting and screens. Quick sketches, AI images, or a few sentences are all fine.
For each idea, answer briefly:
1. **What do people see?** (light, screen, projection, color, motion)
2. **When does it happen?** (which moment of the ride)
3. **Who is it for?** (which persona)
4. **Why does it matter?** (what value it creates for that persona)
Tomorrow we'll look at all the ideas together and choose one direction.
## 5. Context to paste into AI tools
> We are a 3-person team in a university design studio course called "Contextual Mobility UX." The course focuses on designing spatial experiences in vehicles using visuals, video and interaction. Our professor asked us to use low-cost existing car infrastructure — an interior camera, ambient lighting and screens — instead of expensive hardware. We must choose one of two directions and show one shared scenario where three different personas each have a different visual and spatial experience.
>
> Direction 01: A car that knows who is sitting where (via the interior camera) and makes each passenger's personal space visible with light and screens. Personas in one family: Mina (8-month-old baby who needs a quiet, protected zone), Alex (21, whose music and settings should follow him when he changes seats), Margaret (72, a visiting grandmother who needs simple visual guidance).
>
> Direction 02: A car that predicts motion sickness and gives passengers visual cues before turns and braking, using ambient light and screens. Personas in one ride: Alina (20, working on a tablet, sensitive to motion sickness), Emma (9, gets carsick but can't explain it), Leo (25, not sensitive, wants nothing to change).
>
> Give me [3] ideas for visual or interactive video experiences for Direction [01/02]. For each: what people see, when it happens, which persona it's for, and what value it creates.

## C17. Mina’s Concepts: Direction 01 & 02
10 oktyabr · mənbə: Sunny_Concepts_Direction01_02.txt
Sunny's Concept: Seats That Know You
Mina's Concepts: Direction 01 & 02
Team A · Contextual Mobility UX · Design Narrative · ​Oct 10, 2026 ·
Direction 01: Seats That Know You
The interior camera recognizes who sits where, and light and screens make that visible to
everyone in the car. These five ideas develop Direction 01 (Passenger-Centered
Personalization) for one family ride.
They use only what cars already have, as the professor asked:
Interior camera: already used for driver and occupant monitoring
Ambient lighting: colored and zoned
Screens: center display and rear screens
No fingerprint sensor, no new hardware. The five moments follow one ride: recognition →
movement → protection → care for a guest, with a summary for the driver throughout.
Idea 1: Welcome
1. What do people see? When Alex sits down, his personal color glows around his seat
for 2–3 seconds, then fades. His music and settings load at the same moment.
2. When does it happen? At boarding, each time someone takes a seat.
3. Who is it for? Alex (21), and everyone who rides in the car.
4. Why does it matter? The camera is invisible, so people can't tell whether the car
recognized them, or mistook them for someone else. The short glow confirms "the car
knows it's me." It is a confirmation signal, not a permanent decoration, so it doesn't
distract the driver at night.
Idea 2: Move with User
1. What do people see? When Alex changes seats, his color flows across the ceiling from
the old seat to the new one. His video shrinks on the old screen, slides toward the new
seat, and continues on the new screen from the same second. The old seat returns to
neutral.
2. When does it happen? Mid-ride, when the car stops to pick up Margaret and Alex
gives her the front seat.
3. Who is it for? Alex (21).
Page 1 of 5
Sunny's Concept: Seats That Know You
4. Why does it matter? Today, settings stay in the old seat and the next person has to
readjust everything. Here the experience follows the person, not the seat. The flowing
light also fills the 2–3 second gap while the system catches up, so the delay doesn't
feel like an error.
Idea 3: Sleeping Light
1. What do people see? When the camera sees that Mina has fallen asleep, her zone dims
and its light slowly "breathes," rising and falling at a calm rhythm. If Alex, now sitting
next to her, plays his video loudly, the light at the edge of his zone gives a soft shimmer
toward Mina's zone. If he doesn't turn it down, the sound is limited automatically on
Mina's side.
2. When does it happen? Mid-ride, from the moment Mina falls asleep until she wakes up.
3. Who is it for? Mina (8 months), and Alex (21), who moves into the seat next to her.
4. Why does it matter? Mina can't ask for quiet, and Emily can't turn around while
driving. The breathing light says "a baby is sleeping here" without words, so Alex
understands on his own. The light warns first, and the system protects if needed.
Idea 4: Cabin Map
1. What do people see? A small top view of the car on the center screen. Each seat
shows its passenger's color. When Alex moves, his color flows across the map. Mina's
zone breathes on the map just as it does in the cabin.
2. When does it happen? Throughout the ride, as a quiet layer on the center screen.
3. Who is it for? Emily, the driver.
4. Why does it matter? Emily sees who sits where and which zone is protected in one
glance, without turning around. The map is the driver's view of Ideas 1–3, so the whole
system reads as one.
Idea 5: Warm Glow
1. What do people see? Margaret is recognized as a guest, so her face isn't saved. When
the camera notices her hunching and rubbing her arms, a warm amber glow appears
around her seat, and her screen shows one large question: "Would you like to warm
up?" She answers with one tap or a nod, and her seat heating turns on.
2. When does it happen? After Margaret gets in, when the camera sees signs that she is
cold.
3. Who is it for? Margaret (72), who doesn't understand the car's menus and doesn't
want to distract Emily.
Page 2 of 5
Sunny's Concept: Seats That Know You
4. Why does it matter? No menus, and no need to ask the driver. Only her seat warms, so
the shared air stays the same for everyone else. The camera only suggests and
Margaret confirms, so a wrong guess does no harm.
Scenario: one situation, three personas
Situation: an evening ride home. Emily drives, Alex (21) sits in the front, and baby Mina (8
months) sleeps in the back. On the way, they pick up grandmother Margaret (72).
1. Start: Alex sits down, his color glows briefly, and his music starts (Idea 1).
2. Mina falls asleep: her zone dims and starts to breathe (Idea 3).
3. Picking up Margaret: Alex gives the front seat to his grandmother and moves to the
back. His color flows across the ceiling, and his video continues on the rear screen
(Idea 2).
4. A conflict appears: Alex is now next to the sleeping baby. When his sound is too loud,
his zone edge shimmers, and the sound is limited if needed (Idea 3).
5. Margaret: she is recognized as a guest. The camera notices she is cold, a warm glow
appears at her seat, and her screen asks one simple question (Idea 5).
6. Throughout: Emily follows all of this on the cabin map (Idea 4).
Persona What they experience Value
Alex (21) His color glows when he sits; his Continuity, plus awareness of
light and video follow him to the others without being told off
back seat; his zone edge tells him
the baby is asleep
Mina (8 Her zone dims and breathes; the Protected sleep, though she can't
months) sound around her stays low even ask for it
when Alex moves next to her
Margaret A warm glow and one big question Care without menus and without
(72) on her screen; only her seat warms distracting the driver
Emily, the driver, is a supporting character: she sees all of this on the cabin map without
turning around.
Differentiated value
Continuity: the experience belongs to the person, not the seat.
Communication: passengers understand each other through light, without words.
Page 3 of 5
Sunny's Concept: Seats That Know You
Driver focus: one glance replaces turning around.
Trust: the light shows what the invisible camera sees.
Privacy: recognition runs inside the car, nothing is stored, and a profile closes when its
seat is empty.
Today's cars use light and screens mostly for the driver or the whole cabin. This concept
speaks to each passenger in their own zone.
Open points
Decide how distinct colors stay calm enough for the driver at night
Decide what happens when the camera recognizes someone wrongly, and how they
correct it
Direction 02: Motion Comfort
The car shows passengers a visual cue before a movement happens, adapted to each
person's sensitivity. It uses the same existing parts: ambient light, screens and the interior
camera. Personas: Alina (20), Emma (9), Leo (25).
Idea 1: Turn Flow
1. What do people see? 1–2 seconds before a turn, the ambient light flows across the
cabin in the direction of the turn, then returns to neutral.
2. When does it happen? Before turns and braking, using navigation and vehicle data.
3. Who is it for? Everyone in the cabin.
4. Why does it matter? The eyes see the movement coming before the body feels it, so
the body can prepare.
Idea 2: Screen Edge Cues
1. What do people see? When Alina's risk rises, dots at the edges of her tablet move with
the car's motion. They fade when the risk drops.
2. When does it happen? While she reads or works on a winding road.
3. Who is it for? Alina (20), who must finish her slides during the ride.
4. Why does it matter? She can keep working without getting sick.
Idea 3: Emma Window
1. What do people see? The camera notices Emma's head dropping and her body going
still. Her cartoon shrinks, a horizon line appears beside it, and a character says "Let's
count red cars outside!"
Page 4 of 5
Sunny's Concept: Seats That Know You
2. When does it happen? When the camera sees early signs of discomfort.
3. Who is it for? Emma (9), who often gets carsick but can't explain it.
4. Why does it matter? She gets help without having to describe how she feels. The
camera suggests and a parent confirms, so the car doesn't decide alone.
Idea 4: Nothing Changes
1. What do people see? Leo's zone stays normal: no cues on his screen, no changing light
at his seat.
2. When does it happen? The whole ride.
3. Who is it for? Leo (25), who isn't sensitive and enjoys a dynamic ride.
4. Why does it matter? The system is personal, not one-size-fits-all. Leaving someone
alone is also a design decision.
Scenario: one winding road, three personas
Situation: the car enters a winding road. One turn comes up, and each passenger
experiences it differently.
Persona What they experience Value
Alina The light flows toward the turn, and dots She keeps working without
(20) move at the edges of her tablet getting sick
Emma Her cartoon shrinks, a horizon appears, and Help without having to explain
(9) a game invites her to look outside
Leo (25) Nothing changes in his zone His ride stays the way he likes it
Difference from Apple Vehicle Motion Cues: Apple works on one phone, for one person.
This works across the whole cabin and differs per passenger.
Open point
Our evidence (Reuten et al. 2024, Kehl et al.) covers vibration cues; we still need
evidence for visual cues
Page 5 of 5

Team A Brief: Seats That Know You
Team A Brief: Seats That Know You
​Oct 10, 2026 · ​@Sunny
We present Direction 01 on Monday ​Oct 12, 2026 : a car that knows who sits where and
makes each passenger's space visible with light and screens. Please read this whole brief
before you start your part.
1. The concept in short
One sentence: the car's existing interior camera recognizes who sits where, and light and
screens make each passenger's personal space visible to everyone.
The problem we found:
Cars are personalized for one person, the driver, but used by many people.
Memory seats remember a seat slot (buttons 1–2–3), not a person. When someone
changes seats, their settings stay behind.
Passengers who can't control anything themselves, like babies or elderly guests, have
the least control.
What the professor asked (Oct 9):
Drop the fingerprint sensor: too costly and unnecessary.
Use existing infrastructure, the camera plus an interface layer, and no expensive
hardware.
Focus on visual, video and interaction spatial experience.
Choose one direction and show one scenario with three personas, each with a
different experience and different value.
Our answer: we keep the idea of personal zones. The camera replaces the fingerprint, and
light and screens become the main design material.
2. How it works
The camera only decides who is where. Everything else uses parts the car already has.
We add software and a visual layer, not new mechanisms.
1. Camera: the interior camera, already used for driver and occupant monitoring, sees
who sits in each seat.
2. Recognition: it matches the person to a profile. An unknown person is treated as a
guest, and their face is not saved.
Page 1 of 6
Team A Brief: Seats That Know You
3. Personal zone: each seat becomes that person's zone: their color, screen content,
sound and heating.
4. Response through existing parts:
Ambient light: shows the person's color and the zone's state, such as recognized,
sleeping or warming.
Screens: the center screen shows the cabin map; rear screens show each person's
content.
Seat heating and climate: adjust per seat.
Seat position: loaded by the existing seat memory, where the car has power seats.
This already exists in a simpler form. Genesis Face Connect recognizes the driver's face
and applies their seat, mirror, steering wheel, head-up display and infotainment settings
(J.D. Power). We extend the same idea from the driver to every passenger.
Honest limits:
Rear seats usually have no power memory, except in luxury cars. So our visual moments
focus on light and screens, not seat movement.
Separate sound per seat without headphones is still in development (Hyundai's
Separated Sound Zone, 2018). We rely on rear screens with headphones, or the
passenger's own phone.
3. Scenario: one ride, three personas
Situation: an evening ride home. Emily drives, Alex (21) sits in the front, and baby Mina (8
months) sleeps in the back. On the way, they pick up grandmother Margaret (72).
The three personas are all passengers: Alex, Mina and Margaret. Emily, the driver, is a
supporting character.
1. Start: Alex sits down. His color glows briefly at his seat, and his music starts.
2. Mina falls asleep: her zone dims and its light starts to breathe slowly.
3. Picking up Margaret: Alex gives the front seat to his grandmother and moves to the
back. His color flows across the ceiling to his new seat, and his video continues on the
rear screen.
4. A conflict appears: Alex is now next to the sleeping baby. When his sound is too loud,
the edge of his zone shimmers. If he doesn't turn it down, the sound is limited
automatically.
5. Margaret: she is recognized as a guest. The camera notices she is cold, a warm glow
appears at her seat, and her screen asks one simple question: "Would you like to warm
up?"
Page 2 of 6
Team A Brief: Seats That Know You
6. Throughout: Emily follows all of this on the cabin map, without turning around.
The seat change creates the conflict naturally: Alex moves for a real reason, giving his
grandmother the front seat, and ends up next to the sleeping baby.
4. The five visual moments
Each visual serves one purpose: it makes the invisible camera visible, lets passengers
communicate without words, or guides someone who can't use menus.

the driver at night.

for Mina and simple care for Margaret.

6. FAQ
How does the camera make the seat readjust? Isn't that a new mechanism? No. Power
seats with memory already exist; today a memory button or the key fob triggers them. We
only change the trigger: the camera recognizes the person, and the existing seat
controller loads their position. Where a seat has no power memory, we don't move it.
Does every seat need its own music device? No. What moves is the content, not the
hardware: the video or playlist moves from one screen to another. Passengers listen
through the rear screen with headphones, or through their own phone. Separate speakers
per seat are a possible future option, not something we rely on.
Isn't a camera that recognizes faces creepy? Recognition runs inside the car and no
video is stored. Guests are not saved, a profile closes when its seat is empty, and the
camera can be turned off. The Welcome light also shows people what the camera has
Page 4 of 6
Team A Brief: Seats That Know You
recognized.
What if the camera recognizes someone wrongly? The person sees the wrong color at
their seat and corrects it with one tap. For anything uncertain, like Margaret being cold,
the system asks instead of acting.
What does it cost? No new hardware: interior camera, ambient lighting, screens, seat
heating and seat memory are already in many cars. The cost is software and design.
7. Who does what
Before anyone starts: Teammates 1 and 2 agree on one color palette together (for
example Alex = blue, Margaret = amber, Mina = soft lavender). The storyboard and the UI
must use the same colors.
Teammate 1: Storyboard + persona visuals
Storyboard (main task): one frame for each of the six moments in Section 3, using AI
images, sketches or both.
Each frame must clearly show what the light and screens are doing.
Keep the same car, people and colors in every frame, so it reads as one story.
Write one caption under each frame: what happens and who sees what.
Persona cards: one card per persona (Alex, Mina, Margaret) with an image, age, seat,
need and their color.
Seat plan: a top view of who sits where, before and after Margaret gets in, with an
arrow for Alex's move.
Deliverable: 6 storyboard frames with captions, 3 persona cards and 1 seat plan.
Teammate 2: Visual interaction
Cabin Map UI: 3–4 screens of the driver's top-view map:
everyone seated with their colors
Mina asleep, with her zone breathing
Alex moving to the back, his color flowing
Margaret warming up
One short motion piece (5–10 seconds): the light flowing across the ceiling to a new
seat, or the sleeping zone breathing. Figma Smart Animate or After Effects is enough.
Optional: one rear-screen UI, such as Alex's video handoff or Margaret's one-question
card.
Deliverable: the UI screens and one motion clip, ready for the deck.
Teammate 3: Analysis + system
Page 5 of 6
Team A Brief: Seats That Know You
System diagram: camera → recognition → personal zone → light, screen, heating and
seat responses. No fingerprint.
Persona × value slide: based on Section 5.
Privacy and cost slide: based on the FAQ in Section 6.
Feedback check: a short list showing how we answered each point of the professor's
Oct 9 feedback and the earlier Google Chat feedback.
Deliverable: the diagram, the value slide, the privacy and cost slide, and the feedback
check.

## C21. Presentation Script 10 min (EN)
12 oktyabr · mənbə: TeamA_Presentation_Script_10min.txt
Presentation Script
Seats That Know You · Team A · Mina · October 12, 2026
Total: about 9 min 30 s (about 1,100 words at a calm pace), leaving 30 seconds of buffer under the
10-minute limit. If you run late, shorten slides 4, 13 and 15 first.
1 · Cover 0:20
Hello, we are Team A. Today we present the updated version of Direction 01: Seats That Know You, a
car that knows who sits where and makes each passenger's own experience visible.
2 · Research question 0:30
Our research question is simple: how might a shared car give every passenger their own experience,
without disturbing the others? It has two sides: every person gets their own experience, and the
shared cabin stays fair.
3 · Context · precedents 0:35
We mapped what cars already do. Many cars recognize people and respond, like Genesis Face
Connect, Mercedes ambient light, or NIO's assistant. But they all serve either the driver or the whole
cabin. The top-right corner, where each passenger has their own experience and the others can see
it, is empty. That is our design space.
4 · Key findings 0:45
Six findings from existing research. Children in the back are twelve times more distracting than a
phone call. Older adults spend longer with their eyes off the road, and AAA calls this a design
problem, not an age problem. Many car features are never used because setup is too hard. Comfort
is individual. Temperature and music cause real arguments in the car. And Euro NCAP already
rewards rear-seat sensing, so the hardware is coming anyway.
5 · Insight 0:30
This leads to our core tension: personalization is built for one person, but a car is shared by many.
Manual control asks too much. Whole-car control changes too much. Our opportunity is to change
only the zone that needs it.
6 · Concept 0:40
Our concept in one sentence: the camera the car already has knows who sits where, and light and
screens make it visible. We show it in five moments: Welcome, Move with User, Sleeping Light, Warm
Glow, and the Cabin Map. One rule: light speaks to passengers, and the map speaks to the driver.
7 · What changed 0:25
Compared to our last version, two things changed. We replaced the fingerprint sensor with the
existing camera, so there is no touch and no new hardware. And we moved the focus from system
logic to the visible experience. The core idea stayed the same.
8 · Scenario 0:35
Our scenario is one evening ride. Emily drives. Alex, twenty-one, sits in front, and baby Mina sleeps in
the back. On the way they pick up grandmother Margaret, and Alex gives her the front seat. Now Alex
sits next to the sleeping baby. That creates a natural conflict. Each person has their own color: blue,
lavender, and amber.
9 · Storyboard 1 0:35
First, Alex sits down and his blue glows briefly: the car knows him. Second, Mina falls asleep, and her
zone dims and slowly breathes. This works for any sleeping passenger, not only a baby. Third, Alex
moves to the back, and his color and his video move with him.
10 · Storyboard 2 0:35
Fourth, Alex's sound reaches sleeping Mina. His zone edge shimmers as a gentle warning; if he
doesn't lower it, sound is limited only on her side. Fifth, the camera notices Margaret may be cold.
She gets a warm light and one simple question, and one tap warms only her seat. Sixth, Emily sees
all of this on the Cabin Map.
11 · Cabin Map 0:30
This is the driver's view, in four states: everyone seated, Mina asleep, Alex moving back, and
Margaret warming up. Emily understands the cabin in one glance, without turning around. In the
next version we will use icons instead of text, in a small window on the navigation screen.
12 · Comparison 0:35
Here we compare three ways to handle the same moments. With manual control, people redo
settings or give up, and the driver turns around. With whole-car control, one person's need changes
everyone, like turning the sound off for the whole car. Our system changes only the zone that needs
it.
13 · Decision logic 0:40
How does the car decide? People's own saved choices come first. Then every event goes through
four questions in order. Is it about safety? Safety always wins. Does it affect a vulnerable passenger,
like a sleeping baby? Protect them first. Can one zone solve it alone? Change only that zone. Do two
equal passengers want different things? Ask both. And the key rule: if the car is sure, it acts. If not, it
asks.
14 · Value 0:30
The same ride gives each person different value. Alex gets continuity: his experience follows him,
and he becomes aware of others. Mina gets protected sleep. Margaret gets simple, private care,
with no menus and no need to bother the driver.
15 · System · privacy · cost 0:35
The system has four steps: the camera sees who sits where, recognizes a profile or a guest, creates
a personal zone, and the car's existing parts respond. For privacy, everything is processed inside the
car, no video is stored, guests are not saved, and the camera can be turned off. For a baby, the
camera recognizes the child seat, not the face, and a parent confirms. And the cost is low, because
all these parts already exist in cars.
16 · Sketch exploration 0:20
Because our input changed from fingerprint to camera, our sketch exploration restarted. On the left
is the old direction; on the right, the first sketches of the new one. This exploration is still in
progress.
17 · Next stage 0:30
Next, we plan to develop designs like the light language, the screen and map design, and the motion
between seats. These may change as we go. And because the car knows who sits where, the same
system can grow, for example with motion-sickness cues on a rear screen. Thank you.
Timing check
Part Slides Time
Opening and research 1–5 2:40
Concept and scenario 6–11 3:20
Analysis 12–15 2:20
Process and next 16–17 0:50
Total ≈ 9:10–9:30
Tip: Practice once with a timer. If you pass 4:00 at slide 9, skip the extra examples on slides 4 and 15.

## C23. v2 təqdimat skripti (AZ), 15 slayd
18 oktyabr · mənbə: TeamA_Script_10min_AZ.txt
Seats That Know You · Təqdimat skripti
Team A · Contextual Mobility UX · Təqdim edən: Mina · təxminən 10 dəqiqə · 15 slayd
01 · Cover 0:00 – 0:30
Salam, biz Team A-yıq. Bugün "Seats That Know You" layihəmizin yeni versiyasını
təqdim edəcəyik. Fikrimiz sadədir: maşında hər oturacaq bir insanın şəxsi zonasıdır.
İşıq, səs və ekran maşına yox, o insana uyğunlaşır.
02 · After the feedback 0:30 – 1:15
Keçən dəfə bizə beş sual verdiniz. Kameralar harada olacaq? Niyə yalnız bir səfər?
Başqa maşınlarda necə işləyəcək? İşıq, səs və ekran dəqiq necə görünəcək? Və
hamısını eskizlərlə göstərin.
Bu təqdimatda hər suala ayrıca cavab veririk. Sağ tərəfdə hansı slaydda cavab
verdiyimizi görə bilərsiniz.
03 · Concept 1:15 – 1:50
Konsept dəyişməyib: maşın kimin harada oturduğunu bilir və hər oturacaq öz insanına
cavab verir. Bunu üç vasitə ilə edirik.
Light: hər insanın öz rəngi var. Sound: hər başlıqda spiker var, səs öz oturacağında
qalır. Screen: məzmun insanla birlikdə hərəkət edir və başqa oturacağa paylaşıla bilər.
Əsas qaydamız da budur: kiçik şeylərdə maşın özü yumşaq hərəkət edir və bunu geri
qaytarmaq olur, vacib şeylərdə isə əvvəlcə soruşur.
04 · How the car sees every passenger 1:50 – 2:50
İlk sual kameralar idi. Bizim sistemdə iki kamera və bir radar var.
C1 güzgüdəki kameradır, sürücünü və ön sərnişini görür. C2 birinci sıranın üstündə,
tavandadır və arxaya baxır, arxadakı bütün üzləri görür. Radar isə kameranın görmədiyi
yerləri tapır. Məsələn, arxaya baxan uşaq oturacağındakı körpəni onun nəfəsindən hiss
edir.
Bu xəyal deyil. Chevrolet Traverse, Honda Odyssey CabinWatch və Chrysler Pacifica
FamCam bu gün belə kameralardan istifadə edir. Barmaq izi və ya toxunma lazım deyil,
tanınma maşının içində baş verir.
05 · Light language 2:50 – 3:40
İşıq üçün sadə bir dil yaratdıq: rəng kim olduğunu, hərəkət isə nə baş verdiyini göstərir.
Altı pastel rəngimiz var. Qonaq üçün isti ağ rəngdən istifadə edirik. Qırmızı rəngi
istifadə etmirik, çünki qırmızı təhlükəsizlik üçün saxlanılır.
Hərəkətlər belədir: tanınanda bir dəfə parıldayır, yuxuda yavaş nəfəs alır, səs
sərhədində kənar titrəyir, paylaşanda işıq tavan boyunca axır, döngədən əvvəl döngə
tərəfə sürüşür, isinəndə isə soyuq rəngdən isti rəngə keçir.
06 · Light placement 3:40 – 4:15
İşıq havadan yox, maşının özündən gəlir. Üç yerdə görünür: oturacağın kənarında
nazik xətt kimi, qapı panelində və tavan xəttində. Tavan xətti zonaları bir-birinə
bağlayır, paylaşma və yer dəyişmə zamanı istifadə olunur.
Qeyd edək ki, proses zamanı daha yaxşı dizayn həlli tapsaq, yerləşmə dəyişə bilər.
07 · Sound zone 4:15 – 4:50
Hər başlıqda iki kiçik spiker var. Bu, hər insana öz səs zonasını verir. Əgər səs yatan
bir qonşuya çatırsa, əvvəlcə oturacağın kənarı titrəyir. Lazım olsa, səs yalnız o tərəfdə
azaldılır. Buna real nümunə kimi Hyundai-nin Separated Sound Zone texnologiyasını
göstərə bilərik.
08 · Three rides 4:50 – 5:15
Bir səfər əvəzinə üç ssenari hazırladıq. Hər biri sistemin fərqli bir hissəsini yoxlayır:
birincisi paylaşma və şəxsi ekranları, ikincisi yuxu, qayğı və sürücünü, üçüncüsü isə
qonaq vəziyyətini.
09 · Storyboard S1 · Friends on a day trip 5:15 – 6:15
Leo öndə, Yuna arxada oturur. Oturan kimi hər oturacağın kənarı öz rəngində yanır:
Leo teal, Yuna coral. Yuna-nın ekranı ona yarımçıq qalan videosunu davam etdirməyi
təklif edir, oturacağı da artıq 22 dərəcədədir.
Leo ön ekranda "Yuna"ya toxunur və videonu ona göndərir. Yuna-nın ekranında
"Open?" sualı çıxır, onun musiqisi isə yalnız öz oturacağında səslənir. O açanda işıq
tavan boyunca tealdan corala axır, yəni video bir zonadan digərinə keçir.
10 · Storyboard S2 · Night family drive 6:15 – 7:15
Gecədir. Sara maşını sürür, arxada səkkiz yaşlı Noah və körpə Lily oturur. Lily yuxuya
gedir, radar onun sakit nəfəsini hiss edir və onun zonası tutqunlaşır.
Döngəli yolda Noah-nın ekranının kənarındakı işıq döngəni əvvəlcədən göstərir,
beləliklə onun başı gicəllənmir. Noah-nın səsi Lily-yə çatanda səs yalnız Lily tərəfdə
azaldılır. Sara arxaya dönmədən bir baxışla kimin yatdığını ekranda görür. Evə çatanda
isə maşın ona Lily-nin hələ arxada olduğunu xatırladır.
11 · Storyboard S3 · Picking up grandma 7:15 – 8:05
Yolda 72 yaşlı Margaret-i götürürük. O, ön oturacağa oturur və maşın onu qonaq kimi
qəbul edir. İsti ağ halo onu salamlayır, onun haqqında heç nə yadda saxlanılmır.
Ekranda sadəcə "isti" və ya "soyuq" yox, böyük ikonlarla beş sadə səviyyə var, bir
toxunuşla seçilir. Oturacağın işığı soyuqdan istiyə keçir, beləliklə işıq və hiss uyğun
gəlir. Margaret rahatlayır: nə menyuya girdi, nə də sürücüdən nəsə istədi.
12 · Cabin Map 8:05 – 8:40
Sürücü üçün Cabin Map hazırladıq. Bir baxışda kimin harada oturduğunu, kimin
yatdığını və kimin paylaşdığını göstərir. Hər ssenari üçün xəritə fərqlidir.
Sürərkən sadə 2D xəritə istifadə olunur. Maşın dayananda isə interaktiv 3D kabin açılır:
kamera oyundakı kimi oturacaqlar arasında hərəkət edir. Bu hissəni 4D/5D dərsində
inkişaf etdirəcəyik.
13 · Car scale 8:40 – 9:15
Sistem başqa maşınlarda necə işləyəcək? Zonalar eyni qalır, yalnız sensorların sayı
dəyişir. İki nəfərlik maşına bir güzgü kamerası kifayətdir. Ailə SUV-si və minivan güzgü
kamerası, tavan kamerası və radardan istifadə edir. Shuttle avtobusunda isə hər sıra
üçün ayrıca tavan kamerası var.
14 · What we design next 9:15 – 9:50
Bu gün göstərdiyimiz ekranlar hələ placeholder-dir. Motion dərsində welcome ekranını,
videonun kiçilib uçmasını və ekran dizayn sistemini hazırlayacağıq. 4D/5D dərsində isə
işığın hərəkətlərini, səs sərhədini və 3D Cabin Map-i hazırlayacağıq.
15 · Thank you 9:50 – 10:00
Dinlədiyiniz üçün təşəkkür edirik. Suallarınızı məmnuniyyətlə cavablandırarıq.
Məsləhət: vaxt az qalsa, 05 və 06-cı slaydları qısaldın (hər biri 20 saniyə), storyboard-larda isə yalnız
kadrların başlıqlarını deyin.

State-lər (qaralama)
Hər oturacaq üçün
Boş → Tanındı / Qonaq → Aktiv → Yatır / Paylaşır → Boş
Haradan → Haraya Şərt (real maşın) Prototipdə
Boş → Tanındı Tanış üz tapıldı eyni
Boş → Qonaq Üz tapıldı, amma tanış deyil (və ya əmin deyil) eyni
Aktiv → Yatır ~30 s gözlər qapalı + radar sakit nəfəs ~5–10 s gözlər qapalı
Yatır → Aktiv Gözlər açılır / hərəkət edir eyni
Aktiv → Paylaşır Video göndərir və ya qəbul edir düymə ilə (seçimli)
İstənilən → Boş ~10 s kamera və radar heç kimi görmür ~5 s üz görünmür
Necə hazırlayırıq
Bir dizayn mənbəyi, hər yerdə istifadə olunur
Figma: bütün ekranlar və dizayn sistemi
Figma → After Effects (AEUX, pulsuz): ekranlar animasiyaya çevrilir
After Effects → MP4: Figma prototipində və videoda
After Effects → Lottie: kod prototipində
Realistik AI fotolar (storyboard-lar): videonun fonu
Xərc: demək olar ki, pulsuzdur. After Effects məktəbdən.

화면 앞에 앉습니다 내장 카메라가 얼굴을 찾습니다 등록됨 → 이름과 색. 확실하지 않음 → 좌석 빛, 소리, 화면이 바뀝니다 눈을 감으면 수면, 떠나면 빈 좌석
게스트
인식은 컴퓨터 안에서만 처리되고, 게스트는 저장하지 않습니다. 카메라가 확실하지 않으면 게스트로 대합니다. 차 안에서와 같은
규칙입니다.

## C27. Project Scope skripti (AZ)
25 oktyabr · mənbə: Project_Scope_Script_AZ.txt
Project Scope · Təqdimat skripti
Team A · Contextual Mobility UX · Təqdim edən: Mina · təxminən 10 dəqiqə · 19 slayd
01 · Project scope 0:00 – 0:20
Salam. Bu gün Choreograph 3 mərhələsinə uyğun olaraq layihəmizin həcmini təqdim
edəcəyik: nə hazırlayacağıq, hansı alətlərlə və nə vaxta qədər.
02 · Əsas təcrübə 0:20 – 1:00
Bütün işimiz bir cümləyə əsaslanır: oturacaq kim olduğunu bilir və sənə cavab verir.
Bunu üç vasitə ilə göstəririk: işıq, səs və ekran.
Final nəticə üç hissədən ibarət olacaq: insanların özlərinin sınaya biləcəyi interaktiv
prototip, üç ssenarini göstərən video və prosesimizin dokumentasiyası.
03 · Giriş → cavab 1:00 – 1:50
Keçən həftə giriş və cavab əlaqəsini müəyyən etdik. Bu cədvəl hər girişin hansı cavaba
səbəb olduğunu göstərir.
Məsələn, tanınan şəxs oturanda kənar onun rəngində bir dəfə yanır, öz musiqisi
başlayır və ekranda adı çıxır. Tanınmayan şəxsə isə isti ağ rəng və "Welcome" ilə
qonaq kimi cavab verilir.
Yuxunu radar və kamera birlikdə hiss edir. Səs sərhədi üçün isə ayrıca mikrofon lazım
deyil, çünki sistem hər zonanın səs səviyyəsini özü bilir.
04 · Bir oturacağın state-ləri 1:50 – 2:30
Hər oturacağın altı vəziyyəti var: boş, tanındı, qonaq, aktiv, yatır və paylaşır.
Oxlar keçid şərtlərini göstərir. Məsələn, gözlər qapanıb nəfəs sakitləşəndə oturacaq
"yatır" vəziyyətinə keçir. Təxminən 10 saniyə heç kim olmasa, yenidən boş olur.
Prototipdə demo sürətli olsun deyə bu vaxtlar qısadır.
05 · Ekranlar 2:30 – 3:00
Ekranları bir dəfə Figma-da dizayn sistemi kimi qururuq, sonra After Effects-də
animasiya edirik.
Səkkiz element var: dizayn sistemi, Welcome və qonaq ekranı, Continue, Share,
"Open?" kartı və videonun uçması, istilik, Cabin Map 2D və xatırlatma.
06 · İşıq və səs 3:00 – 3:30
İşıq üçün əvvəlcə light spec hazırlayırıq: hər vəziyyətin rəngi, müddəti və ritmi.
Sonra işıq animasiyalarını realistik fotolarımızın üzərində After Effects-də edirik:
welcome pulse, yuxu nəfəsi, sound edge, share axını, motion cue, warmth və qapı
işığı. Səs üçün hər oturacağın öz musiqisi olur və yatan birinin yanında azalır.
07 · Prototip və video 3:30 – 3:55
Üç əsas nəticə: kameralı interaktiv prototip (4D/5D), ssenari videosu (Motion) və
dokumentasiya. Ssenari videosu təxminən 2–3 dəqiqə olacaq. Ekran klipləri 3–6
saniyə, işıq animasiyaları isə 2–4 saniyəlik loop-lardır. Hər həftə gördüyümüz işi və
qərarları qeyd edirik.
08 · İş axını 3:55 – 4:25
Hər şey bir dizayn mənbəyindən çıxır. Figma-dakı ekranlar AEUX ilə After Effects-ə
köçürülür. Oradan MP4 kliplər videoya, Lottie animasiyaları isə koda gedir.
Bir şeyi Figma-da dəyişəndə video və prototip uyğun qalır, bu da bizə vaxt qazandırır.
09 · Ekran = oturacaq 4:25 – 5:00
Prototipdə ekran oturacağın rolunu oynayır. İnsan ekranın qarşısında oturur, daxili
kamera üzü tapır və tanıyır.
Tanış adam adını və rəngini görür, əmin olmadıqda isə qonaq kimi qəbul edilir. Bu,
maşındakı qərar qaydamızla eynidir. Tanıma yalnız kompüterin içində olur, qonaqlar
yadda saxlanılmır.
10 · Kamera bunu edə bilərmi? 5:00 – 5:50
Bunu araşdırdıq. Cavab: bəli, nəzarət olunan şəraitdə.
Əsas planımız Human kitabxanasıdır: brauzerdə, serversiz işləyir və daxili qeydiyyatı
var. Alternativ olaraq face-api.js-i də sınayırıq. MediaPipe isə yalnız üzü tapır, kim
olduğunu bilmir.
11 · Nəyi yoxlayırıq 5:50 – 6:40
Prototiplə yoxladığımız sual budur: insanlar izahsız, yalnız işıq və ekrandan oturacağın
onları tanıdığını anlayırmı?
Test planımız: gələn həftə kamera və komanda testi, 11-ci həftədə datası qeyd
olunmayan istifadəçilərlə test, 13-cü həftədə isə hər şeyin birlikdə işlədiyini yoxlayan
final test.
Davam etmə qaydamız: 10 cəhddən ən azı 9-u düzgün olmalı, yad adama isə heç vaxt
səhv ad verilməməlidir.
12 · Qrafik 6:40 – 7:10
Showcase-ə qədər yeddi həftə var. Syllabus-a uyğun olaraq Choreograph-dan sonra
üç həftə prototip, iki həftə Experience mərhələsi gəlir. 12/7-də isə final showcase
olacaq.
13 · Week 9 7:10 – 7:30
Bu həftə: giriş-cavab cədvəli, state-lər və scope. Motion-da işıq timing eskizlərinə
başlayırıq. Kamera üçün alətləri artıq araşdırmışıq.
14 · Week 10 7:30 – 7:50
Dizayn sistemi və Welcome ekranı hazır olur. Light spec tamamlanır, Welcome pulse
animasiyası hazırlanır. Kamera alətlərini test edirik, prototipin ilk versiyası üzü tanıyır
və komanda daxilində yoxlanılır.
15 · Week 11 7:50 – 8:15
Əsas ekranlar hazırlanır. Open? kartı və share axını animasiya olunur. Prototip bizim
dizaynla işləyir və datası qeyd olunmayan istifadəçilərlə ilk testi edirik. Vaxt çatmasa,
Cabin Map növbəti həftəyə keçə bilər.
16 · Week 12 8:15 – 8:40
Qalan ekranlar tamamlanır və dizayn dondurulur. Fotoların üzərində işıq animasiyaları
və şəxsi səs əlavə olunur. Prototip işıq və səs vəziyyətlərini göstərir.
17 · Week 13 8:40 – 9:00
Testlərə əsasən düzəlişlər edirik, son işıq animasiyalarını tamamlayırıq. Prototip tam
şəkildə yoxlanılır və video montajına başlayırıq.
18 · Week 14 9:00 – 9:25
Ssenari videosu və dokumentasiya hazır olur.
19 · Week 15 9:25 – 10:00
12/7-də final showcase: qonaqlar prototipi özləri sınayır, yanında ssenari videosu
oynayır.
Dinlədiyiniz üçün təşəkkür edirik. Suallarınızı məmnuniyyətlə cavablandırarıq.
Məsləhət: vaxt az qalsa, həftə slaydlarında (13–19) yalnız hər sütundan bir əsas işi deyin.

## C29. Overview skripti (AZ): üç dərs birləşəndə
26 oktyabr · mənbə: Overview_Script_AZ.txt
Seats That Know You · Ümumi baxış ·
Skript
Team A · Mina Azhar · Alina · təxminən 10 dəqiqə · 16 slayd
01 · Seats That Know You 0:00 – 0:25
Salam, biz Team A-yıq. Bu həftədən üç dərs birləşdiyi üçün layihəmizi başdan qısaca
təqdim edəcəyik.
Layihəmizin adı “Seats That Know You”-dur: hər oturacaq bir insanın şəxsi zonasıdır,
işıq, səs və ekran o insana uyğunlaşır.
02 · Brief və sual 0:25 – 1:05
Bir neçə istiqamət arasından bizim seçdiyimiz sual belədir: hərəkət edən məkan hər
sərnişini necə tanıya və ona necə cavab verə bilər?
Bir maşında həm regular userlər, həm də irregular userlər olur. Amma bu gün sistem
əsasən yalnız sürücünü tanıyır, sərnişinlər isə hər şeyi əl ilə tənzimləyir və ya
sürücüdən xahiş edir. Bizim fikrimiz oturacağın hər bir sərnişini tanımasıdır.
03 · Proses 1:05 – 1:40
Əvvəlcə Hyundai UX Studio Seoul-u ziyarət edib öz səfərlərimizi müşahidə etdik.
Frame mərhələsində fokusumuzu sürücüdən sərnişinlərə keçirdik.
Imagine mərhələsində hər üzv öz ideyasını hazırladı və üç istiqamət çıxdı. Sonra
istiqamətimizi seçdik. Choreograph mərhələsində ssenariləri, storyboard-ları və state-
ləri hazırladıq. Bu həftə scope təqdimatımızı etdik və artıq real dizayn prosesinə
başlamışıq.
04 · Feedback-dən sonra 1:40 – 2:15
Feedback-lərlə ideya inkişaf etdi. Başlanğıcda barmaq izi fikri ilə başlamışdıq, amma
xərc və dizayn uyğunluğu baxımından onu kamera və radar ilə əvəz etdik.
Bir səfər əvəzinə üç ssenari hazırladıq. Fokusu sistem məntiqindən görünən
təcrübəyə keçirdik və sistemin istənilən ölçüdə maşında necə işlədiyini göstərdik.
05 · Konsept 2:15 – 2:50
Əsas cümləmiz: oturacaq kim olduğunu bilir və sənə cavab verir.
Bunu üç vasitə ilə edirik. Light: hər insanın öz rəngi var. Sound: səs öz oturacağında
qalır. Screen: məzmun insanla gedir və paylaşıla bilər. Sistem sadə bir prinsiplə işləyir:
kiçik məsələlərdə maşın özü yumşaqca tənzimləyir və bunu asanlıqla geri qaytarmaq
olur, daha vacib məsələlərdə isə əvvəlcə sərnişindən soruşur.
06 · Sistem 2:50 – 3:30
Maşın sərnişinləri iki kamera və bir radarla görür. C1 güzgüdədir, öndəkiləri görür. C2
tavandadır, arxaya baxır. Radar kor nöqtələri və arxaya baxan körpəni nəfəsindən tapır.
Bu texnologiya bu gün Genesis Face Connect, Subaru DriverFocus və Honda
CabinWatch kimi nümunələrdə mövcuddur.
07 · Üç səfər 3:30 – 3:55
Üç ssenari hazırladıq, hər biri fərqli ehtiyacı göstərir: dostların paylaşması, gecə yatan
körpə və qonaq nənə.
08 · S1 · Friends on a day trip 3:55 – 4:40
Leo öndə, Yuna arxada oturur, oturacaqlar onların rəngində yanır. Yuna-nın ekranı
videosunu davam etdirməyi təklif edir.
Leo “Yuna”ya toxunub videonu göndərir. Yuna-nın ekranında “Open?” çıxır, açanda
işıq bir userin rəngindən digər userin rənginə axır, yəni paylaşma maşının içində
vizual olaraq görünür.
09 · S2 · Night family drive 4:40 – 5:25
Gecə Lily yuxuya gedir, radar nəfəsini hiss edir və onun zonası tutqunlaşır. Döngəli
yolda işıq Noah-ya döngəni əvvəlcədən göstərir.
Noah-nın səsi yalnız Lily tərəfdə azalır. Sara dönmədən kimin yatdığını görür, evə
çatanda maşın Lily-ni xatırladır.
10 · S3 · Picking up grandma 5:25 – 6:05
Margaret öndə oturur və maşın onu qonaq kimi qəbul edir: isti ağ halo, heç nə yadda
saxlanılmır.
Böyük ikonlarla 5 istilik səviyyəsindən birini seçir, oturacaq işığı soyuqdan istiyə keçir.
Nə menyu var, nə də sürücüdən xahiş.
11 · Light language 6:05 – 6:40
İşıq üçün sadə bir dil yaratdıq: rəng kim olduğunu, hərəkət nə baş verdiyini deyir.
Altı pastel rəng var, qonaq üçün isti ağ. Altı hərəkət var: welcome, yuxu, sound edge,
share, motion cue və warmth. İşıq oturacağın kənarında, qapıda və tavan xəttindədir.
12 · Səs və Cabin Map 6:40 – 7:15
Hər başlıqda iki kiçik spiker var. Səs qonşuya çatanda əvvəlcə kənar titrəyir, sonra səs
yalnız o tərəfdə azalır.
Sürücü üçün Cabin Map var: dönmədən kimin harada oturduğunu və kimin yatdığını
görür.
13 · Car scale 7:15 – 7:45
Sistem istənilən ölçüdə işləyir. Zonalar eyni qalır, yalnız sensorların sayı dəyişir: iki
nəfərlik maşından avtobusa qədər.
14 · Hər dərsdə nə hazırlayırıq 7:45 – 8:45
İndi əsas hissə: hər dərsdə nə hazırlayacağıq.
Design Narrative-də təcrübənin məntiqi (giriş-cavab və state-lər), Figma-da ekran
dizaynı, light spec və dokumentasiya.
Motion-da ekran animasiyaları, fotoların üzərində işıq animasiyaları və təxminən 2–3
dəqiqəlik ssenari videosu.
4D/5D-də kamera ilə tanıyan interaktiv prototip: state-lər, işıq və səs cavabı.
15 · İnteraktiv prototip 8:45 – 9:30
Prototipdə ekran oturacağın rolunu oynayır. İnsan oturur, kamera üzü tapır, tanış
adamın adı və rəngi çıxır, əmin olmadıqda isə qonaq kimi cavab verir.
Alət olaraq brauzerdə işləyən Human kitabxanasını, alternativ olaraq face-api.js-i
istifadə edirik. Yoxladığımız sual: insanlar izahsız oturacağın onları tanıdığını
anlayırmı?
16 · Qrafik 9:30 – 10:00
11/2-dən prototip mərhələsi başlayır, 12/7-də final showcase olacaq.

## C30. Research Report: Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype
oktyabr · mənbə: 020ca911-Can_We_Build_a_Face-Recognition_Prototype_Research_Report.txt
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research Report
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype?
Research Report
Team A · Contextual Mobility UX · Design Narrative · October 2026
Summary
This report evaluates whether a small design team can build a prototype in which a laptop
camera recognizes an enrolled person (for example, Mina) and shows Hi Mina, shows
Welcome for an unrecognized visitor, and triggers a seat light and screen response. It
combines two supplied research papers and a one-page extract of a third with current
documentation for browser-based tools and two automotive examples. A proof of
concept is feasible without a dedicated server: a browser can use the webcam with
permission, and face descriptors can be compared locally. Performance will drop when
faces are small, poorly lit, turned away, or partly hidden, and face detection alone does not
say who someone is.
Feasible: free browser tools can detect faces and match identities locally. We
recommend testing Human first, and using face-api.js and MediaPipe (the starting
points we were given) as the comparison.
Reliable only in controlled conditions: a front-facing camera, soft light from the front,
close faces (about 0.3 to 0.8 m), and heads turned toward the camera.
A real baby is not a realistic enrolled user: use a consenting teammate, or trigger
Mina's profile by hand and say so.
Decision rule: build on the recognition tool only if the enrolled-person test gives at
least 9 correct matches in 10 and the stranger test never shows a wrong name.
Otherwise, use a manual trigger.
We have not yet run controlled webcam tests. Every reliability statement here is an
expectation from papers and documentation, not a measurement from our laptop. Section
8 is the plan for collecting our own results.
Question and scope
Research question: can we build a face-recognition prototype in which a laptop camera
shows Hi Mina for a known face and Welcome for an unknown face, with the seat light and
screen responding?
Page 1 of 15
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research Report
Target interaction: (1) a camera detects one or more faces; (2) each face is compared with
locally enrolled reference data; (3) a match above a chosen confidence threshold shows
Hi Mina; (4) a non-match shows Welcome; (5) the screen and seat light respond.
Scope: feasibility, tool choice, reliability, difficulty and time, showcase setup, privacy,
automotive precedents, and how to test. The prototype is a design showcase, not a
security or access-control system, so a false match is an interaction error and not proof of
identity.
Our scenario: Direction 01 (Seats That Know You), where the car recognizes who sits
where and each passenger's space responds with light and screens.
Evidence used: we separate (a) claims made by the supplied papers, (b) current tool and
vendor documentation, and (c) our own engineering recommendations. The papers are
FaceNet (Schroff et al., 2015) and Wright et al. (2008), plus Li et al.'s A Review of Face
Recognition Technology (2020), whose supplied PDF is only a one-page reference-list
extract and is used for bibliographic context only. The documentation comes from the
Human, face-api.js, and MediaPipe projects, MDN Web Docs, Genesis, and Subaru.

Detection is not recognition. A multi-face detector can draw boxes around two people
without knowing which one is Mina. Identity matching is a separate stage and must be
tested separately, especially when people overlap or a face is partly hidden. Facial-
expression classification is also separate from identity recognition, and it is outside the
first version.
Informal observation. In an informal look at the face-api.js demo, it appeared able to
detect faces and tell them apart, while its emotion and expression predictions seemed
less convincing. This is a qualitative impression, not a test. The prototype only needs
identity, so emotion recognition is out of scope.
What without a server means. A browser application can process webcam frames on the
laptop without uploading them. However, a hosted demo still downloads its code and
model files, and the host may see ordinary connection data. For a stronger guarantee,
download the code and models in advance, serve them from localhost, disconnect the
network for the final test, and inspect the code for image uploads, analytics, or remote
calls. Local processing is a property of the implementation, not of running in a browser.
Page 3 of 15
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research Report
Practical caveat. The face-api.js demo is an older project, so we treat it as a starting
example and not as a dependable modern component. If it does not load in the current
browser, run the repository examples locally and keep the JavaScript files and model
weights in the project folder. Camera access needs the user's permission and a secure
context such as HTTPS or localhost.
2. Key studies
FaceNet (Schroff, Kalenichenko & Philbin, 2015)
Setup: a deep neural network maps each face image to a compact list of 128 numbers (an
embedding). Images of the same person should be close together, and images of different
people far apart. Recognition then compares an observed face with stored examples.
Finding: the paper reports 99.63% accuracy on the LFW benchmark with face alignment
(98.87% with a plain center crop) and 95.12% on YouTube Faces. The model trained on 100
to 200 million face images of about 8 million people. Pose and illumination are described as
long-standing challenges, and the paper shows examples it can handle. It also measures
how face size affects accuracy for its model (NN1, at a 0.1% false-accept rate):
Face thumbnail Correct-match rate
40×40 pixels 37.8%
80×80 pixels 79.5%
120×120 pixels 84.5%
160×160 pixels 85.7%
256×256 pixels 86.4%
The model also held up under image compression, scoring 81.4% at JPEG quality 20
against 86.5% at quality 90. Smaller models cost accuracy: the tiny mobile model scored
51.9% against 89.4% for the largest.
For us: the practical recipe is to store one or more descriptors for Mina, compare each
new face against them, and return unknown when no match is close enough. Four rules
follow. First, thresholds belong to the model: FaceNet chose 1.242 on LFW, while the face-
api.js example uses 0.6, so we set our own threshold from our test distances. Second, aim
for faces at least 120 pixels wide, treat 80 pixels as the floor, and record the face-box
Page 4 of 15
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research Report
width in every test. Third, use the library's full detect, align, and describe pipeline,
because alignment raised LFW accuracy. Fourth, expect lower numbers from compact
browser models, and treat the headline results as an upper bound, since they come from
still photos and not from a webcam in a classroom.
Wright et al. (2008): Robust Face Recognition via Sparse Representation
Setup: faces are classified as sparse combinations of training images. The paper tests
variation in expression and lighting, as well as occlusion and corruption. Its main
experiments use frontal faces, and the method assumes detection, cropping, and
normalization are already done. It does not explicitly account for pose.
Finding: with sunglasses (about 20% of the face hidden) it scored 87.0% against 70.0%
for a standard baseline, and with scarves (about 40% hidden) it fell to 59.5%. For a block
of the face hidden, it scored 98.5% at 30%, 90.3% at 40%, and 65.3% at 50%. Training sets
with wider variation in lighting and expression made recognition more robust, while sets
of near-identical images were weaker (their experiments used about 7 images per
person). The paper also treats rejecting unknown people as a separate problem, because
a generic face can resemble several enrolled people.
For us: this method is not available in free browser tools, so we use only the lessons. A
recognizer cannot match a face the detector missed or one seen from a very different
angle, so the camera must face the seats. We should enrol 5 to 8 varied photos per person
(different lighting, expressions, and glasses on and off). For strangers, we should test a
rule where a name is accepted only if the best match is under the threshold and clearly
better than the runner-up, and otherwise the screen shows Welcome. That rule is our
adaptation of the paper's idea, not a result from it.
Li et al. (2020): A Review of Face Recognition Technology
This review surveys recognition methods, development stages, evaluation datasets, and
real-world challenges. It is a map of the field and not a test of our prototype, and it
reinforces that reported performance depends on the algorithm, the data, and the
evaluation conditions. Our supplied PDF is only a one-page reference-list extract, so we
attribute no detailed claims to it.
Taken together, the papers show that face recognition is technically established, but
benchmark results are not guaranteed real-world performance. They justify testing the
whole pipeline under our actual camera, room, seating distance, and visitors.

With two people, the application should compute a descriptor for every detected face,
compare each with the enrolled identities, and show a separate result for each box. For a
single-seat interaction, define a priority rule, for example responding only to the face
nearest the center of the frame, or asking people to approach one at a time. Do not
silently assume the largest face is the intended visitor.
4. How hard is it?
For a student prototype the difficulty is moderate if the team adapts a working example
and keeps the interaction simple. It becomes much harder if the team trains a model, tries
to guarantee performance across arbitrary conditions, or builds a custom hardware link
from scratch.
Stage Estimated Skills and output
effort
1. Run an existing demo 1 to 3 hours Browser permissions and basic
troubleshooting. Verify camera access and
face boxes
2. Enrol Mina and match 0.5 to 1.5 days Basic JavaScript. Capture several consented
faces reference images, compute descriptors,
label matches
3. Add screen states 2 to 4 hours HTML, CSS, JavaScript. Show Hi Mina,
Welcome, and a no-face state

Our planning estimate is about 2 to 5 working days for a convincing proof of concept, if
someone on the team can modify basic JavaScript and the lamp hardware is available.
This is an estimate, not a measured duration, and a beginner team may need longer,
especially for local deployment and physical-device communication. No custom model
training should be necessary.
Go or no-go rule: in a bright room at the intended seating distance, the enrolled-person
test should give at least 9 correct matches in 10 attempts, and a person who is not
enrolled should never be shown a wrong name. If either fails, use a manual profile trigger
as the reliable fallback instead of pretending recognition is dependable.
5. Showcase setup and physical outputs
Camera and seating
Use the built-in webcam or an external webcam at about eye level, centered above the
screen.
Start with the participant seated or standing about 0.5 to 0.8 m away, facing the
camera, with soft light from the front and a plain background. This is a practical
starting point and not a universal model limit. For comparison, Genesis instructs users
to register at 0.3 to 0.8 m for its dedicated automotive system.
Mark a target zone on the floor or seat and test it before the showcase.
Screen
Page 8 of 15
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research Report
A normal laptop display is enough. Use large, high-contrast text and a clear state
change: Hi Mina for a stable enrolled match, Welcome for a detected but unrecognized
face, and a neutral idle state when no face is present.
Do not change the message on every video frame. Require a stable match over several
consecutive frames and use a short cooldown before retriggering the lamp.
Lamp or seat light
The simplest reliable setup is a low-voltage LED strip or lamp controlled by a
microcontroller (for example an Arduino-compatible board or an ESP32) over USB
serial. The browser app sends a small state command such as KNOWN, GUEST, or IDLE,
and the microcontroller maps it to light color or brightness.
A compatible smart lamp can also work, but its API and local-network behavior must be
verified. Avoid mains-voltage DIY wiring unless a qualified person does it.
Suggested mapping: an enrolled face gives soft warm light and Hi Mina, an unknown
face gives neutral or cool light and Welcome, and no face gives a dim idle light.
Provide a manual button or keyboard fallback so the showcase does not depend
entirely on recognition.
Mina and the baby
If Mina represents a baby in a rear-facing seat, we should not assume the laptop camera
can identify her. The camera may not see her face, and infant recognition is a separate,
harder problem. For the showcase, use a consenting adult or teammate to represent the
enrolled user, or trigger the baby's profile manually and describe that limitation honestly.
6. Privacy and offline operation
A fully local implementation is technically possible, but it must be designed deliberately.
MDN documents that browser camera access requires permission and a secure context.
The permission prompt is not evidence that the application keeps frames private,
because that depends on whether the code sends data elsewhere.
Download the HTML and JavaScript dependencies and the model files in advance, and
store them in the project folder.
Run the application locally (for example on localhost) and test with Wi-Fi disconnected
after all assets are available.
Inspect the code for fetch(), XMLHttpRequest, WebSocket, analytics, remote logging,
or image-upload endpoints, and remove anything unnecessary.
Page 9 of 15
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research Report
Keep enrolled descriptors in local memory or local files only. Human's Face ID demo
already stores registered faces in the browser's local IndexedDB. Prefer descriptors
over retaining full face photos, but remember that descriptors are still biometric data.
Never store a guest. An unknown face shows Welcome and nothing is saved.
Do not store or record webcam video unless it is essential and participants have
explicitly agreed.
Obtain informed consent from anyone enrolled or filmed, explain what the system
does, and offer a way to opt out and delete their enrolment. Delete all enrolled data
after the showcase.
Use a conservative unknown fallback, and do not use the prototype for access control,
attendance, grading, or other consequential decisions.
Offline test: load the local application and models, disconnect the laptop from the
network, grant camera permission, and confirm that recognition and the lamp still work. If
the app needs to fetch a model or library from a CDN, it is not yet fully offline. We should
also state openly that the initial download of hosted tools may involve network requests
even if the later video processing is local.
7. Automotive precedents
Genesis Face Connect. Genesis describes it as a system that identifies a registered
driver, can unlock the vehicle, and adjusts driver-specific settings such as seat and
steering position, head-up display, mirrors, and infotainment. On the GV60 it uses a near-
infrared (NIR) camera to support recognition in cloudy or dark conditions. Its owner's
manual specifies a registration distance of 0.3 to 0.8 m. Genesis reportedly registers up to
two faces per car and stores the face data encrypted within the vehicle.
Subaru DriverFocus. Subaru says DriverFocus can recognize multiple drivers (up to five
profiles) and remember preferences such as seat position, climate settings, and mirror
position on supported models. Its privacy notice describes the optional profile feature as
generating a computer-produced code associated with facial features for identity
verification, and says users can delete a profile. Subaru also notes that effectiveness can
be affected by clothing worn on the head or face.
What this means for us: both examples show the value of explicit enrolment,
personalization, and deletion controls, and both lead to a visible change in the seat and
screen. Both use dedicated sensors with controlled camera placement, recognize only the
driver, and store just a few profiles. Our concept extends recognition to every seat and
Page 10 of 15
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research Report
our prototype uses an ordinary webcam, so their performance claims cannot be
transferred to a laptop. The prototype should feel like a personalized environment and not
just a camera demo.
8. Demos and test protocol
The task asks us to try one or two demos on a laptop. We explored the face-api.js demo
informally (section 1), but controlled tests across lighting, distance, pose, glasses, and
multiple people have not been completed. The samples and protocol below let us verify
performance under the real showcase conditions. We will report the results once the
tests are run, and an untested condition is never counted as a success.
Sample A: face-api.js recognition demo (the starting point we were given). Open
https://justadudewhohacks.github.io/face-api.js/ and confirm that it loads and asks for
webcam permission. If the sample has reference-image or labelled-descriptor controls,
enrol one consenting participant and check whether repeated views match consistently.
If the hosted demo is broken or lacks enrolment, run the repository's browser examples
locally.
Sample B: Human Face ID and Face Match (recommended). Open the demos from
https://github.com/vladmandic/human, enrol people with a few photos each, and compare
against Sample A under the same conditions. This is the better first test because it
includes a local enrolment flow, but it has not been verified on our laptop.
Optional: MediaPipe Face Detector. Open https://mediapipe-
studio.webapps.google.com/demo/face_detector to check raw detection with one and
then two people. It is not an identity test and cannot verify Hi Mina.
Test matrix. For each condition run at least 10 short trials. Record true matches, wrong
names, unknown outcomes, and missed detections. Also record the face-box width in
pixels and, if the tool shows them, the distance to the best match and to the second-best
match, because those numbers let us set the threshold. Never count a displayed name as
success unless it was the correct name. Keep the laptop, camera angle, screen brightness,
and lighting fixed except for the variable being tested.
# Condition Pass looks like
1 Bright room, 0.5 to 0.8 m, one enrolled person At least 9 of 10 correct
2 Dim room, same distance Note how it fails
