A false match is more serious than an unknown result, so prefer a conservative threshold
and let the system say Welcome when uncertain.
9. Design rules for the prototype
1. Treat unknown as a valid result. Show Welcome when no match is clearly close enough
(the FaceNet and Wright et al. lessons on thresholds and rejecting strangers).
2. Require a stable match. Wait for several consecutive frames, then use a short
cooldown before the lamp changes, to avoid flicker.
3. Match every face independently. Compute a descriptor per detected face and define
a priority rule for single-seat interactions.
4. Keep processing and enrolled data local. Store descriptors and not photos, never
store guests, and record no video by default.
5. Always provide a manual fallback. A key press or button should trigger each profile if
recognition fails.
Page 12 of 15
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research Report
6. Do not claim baby recognition. Use a teammate or a manual trigger for Mina, and say
so.

Limits of this report:
No controlled webcam tests have been run, so there are no results from our own setup
yet.
The FaceNet and Wright et al. results come from lab photo sets and large trained
models, and the FaceNet face-size numbers belong to one model and cannot be
assumed for face-api.js or Human.
Page 13 of 15
Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research Report
Some sources are vendor documentation and manuals, which describe dedicated
automotive hardware.
The time estimates in section 4 and the lamp setup in section 5 are our
recommendations and were not sourced or tested.
Our supplied copy of the Li et al. review is only a reference-list extract.
We found no source on recognizing two people at once, so this must be tested.
11. Conclusion and recommendation
Yes: the proposed prototype is realistic as a design proof of concept. The most practical
route is to test Human's Face ID and Face Match demos first for local identity matching,
compare them with the face-api.js demo we were given, use MediaPipe only to check
detection and multi-face handling, and connect the resulting states to a simple screen
interface and a low-voltage lamp controller. A basic version can likely be built in several
working days by someone comfortable with basic JavaScript, provided the hardware is
available and the team does not try to train a new model.
The main technical risk is not whether a face can be detected in ideal conditions. It is
whether the system can make stable, conservative identity decisions when light,
distance, pose, and the number of people change. The main project risk is assuming that
an online demo is automatically offline, private, or ready to control hardware. Before
committing the next weeks, we should run the samples, complete the test matrix, and
demonstrate the full chain from camera to screen to lamp under the real showcase
conditions. We should treat unknown as a valid result, keep processing local, and provide
a manual fallback.

## C31. Face-Recognition Prototype: Brief
oktyabr · mənbə: 5bbaca1c-Face-Recognition_Prototype_Brief.txt
Face-Recognition Prototype: Brief
Face-Recognition Prototype: Brief
Team A · Contextual Mobility UX · Design Narrative · October 2026
Bottom line
Yes. A showcase where a laptop camera shows Hi Mina for an enrolled face and Welcome
for anyone else, with the seat light and screen responding, is realistic with free browser
tools and no server. It works only in controlled conditions, and a real baby cannot be the
enrolled user. We have not yet run controlled webcam tests, so the reliability points below
are expectations from papers and documentation, not measurements from our laptop.
Answers to the five questions
Question Answer
Is it possible? Yes. Human (its Face ID and Face Match demos) is
the best first test, and face-api.js, the starting
point we were given, is the comparison. MediaPipe
only detects faces and cannot name them.
How reliable is it? Good with a front-facing camera, soft front light,
and faces about 0.3 to 0.8 m away and turned
toward the camera. Worse in dim light, far away,
from the side, with sunglasses, and for infants. Aim
for faces at least 120 pixels wide in the camera
image.
How hard is it? Moderate. It needs basic JavaScript, a local web
server, and a simple lamp controller. Our estimate
is 2 to 5 working days, with no model training.
What does the showcase need? A webcam at eye level, a soft lamp in front of the
faces, 0.5 to 0.8 m distance, a marked zone, a laptop
screen for the Hi Mina and Welcome states, a low-
voltage lamp driven by a microcontroller, and a
manual fallback.

Face-Recognition Prototype: Brief
Question Answer
Can it stay private? Yes. Download the code and models, serve them
from localhost, test with the network off, store
descriptors instead of photos, never store guests,
get consent, and delete everything afterward.
Evidence in one line
FaceNet (2015) and Wright et al. (2008) show that recognition is technically established,
but their benchmark results are not guarantees for a webcam in a classroom. Genesis
Face Connect and Subaru DriverFocus rely on dedicated cameras (infrared in the cases
we checked), recognize only the driver, and store a few profiles.
Decision rule
Build on the tool only if the bright-room test gives at least 9 correct matches in 10 and the
stranger test never shows a wrong name. Otherwise, trigger each profile by hand and say
so openly.
Design rules
Treat unknown as a valid result and show Welcome when no match is clearly close
enough.
Require a stable match over several frames before the lamp changes.
Match every face independently, and define a priority rule for one seat.
Keep processing and enrolled data local, and never store guests.
Provide a manual fallback.
Do not claim baby recognition. Use a teammate or a manual trigger for Mina.
Next steps
1. Run the Human and face-api.js demos in the showcase room: bright versus dim light,
near versus far, and one versus two people.
2. Record the results, including the face-box width in pixels.
3. Apply the decision rule.
Page 2 of 3
Face-Recognition Prototype: Brief
4. Connect the screen and lamp, then rehearse with the network off.
Full details are in the report, Can We Build a Face-Recognition Prototype? Research
Report.
Page 3 of 3

## C32. Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing: Research Summary
oktyabr · mənbə: 332e84fb-Motion_Comfort_for_Rear-Screen_Viewing_Research_Summary.txt
Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing: Research Summary
Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing:
Research Summary
Team A · Contextual Mobility UX · Design Narrative · October 2026
Summary
Passengers who watch a rear screen can feel sick because their eyes see a still image
while their body feels the car turn, brake, and accelerate. This article reviews what
research says about reducing that with screen content, peripheral cues, and ambient
light, using no new mechanism in the car, and turns it into experiments for our 4D/5D and
Motion classes.
Best supported: peripheral cues that announce an upcoming maneuver, and keeping a
view of the world in the passenger's periphery.
Promising but unproven: ambient light (the studies combine it with other measures)
and motion-aware video (a design hypothesis).
Rules that run through all of it: keep cues simple and steady instead of using raw road
footage, tailor them to the passenger because susceptibility varies, and measure
sickness, situation awareness, and comfort separately.
The evidence is small and partly conflicting, so every idea here is a hypothesis to test.
Question and scope
Research question: can visual content and light cues inside a moving car be designed to
reduce passengers' motion-sickness symptoms or improve their perceived comfort while
they look at a rear screen?
Scope: screen-based motion graphics, video treatments, peripheral visual cues,
anticipatory cues, and ambient lighting. No new physical mechanism is needed.
Prototypes are made in tools such as After Effects or TouchDesigner, or as short video
studies.
Our scenario: Direction 01 (Seats That Know You). Section 7 applies the research to it and
section 8 turns it into experiments.
Page 1 of 13
Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing: Research Summary
1. Why a rear screen causes sickness
Carsickness is associated with a mismatch between the motion a person senses and the
motion they expect. Passengers who use a display are at higher risk than drivers, because
the display pulls their eyes away from the road and removes the cues that let them
anticipate turns. A rear screen is a strong example: the eyes lock onto an image that does
not move while the body feels every maneuver.
A 2025 TU Delft study (Irmak, de Winkel & Happee) notes, citing survey work, that 46% of
passengers get motion sick, especially when their eyes are off the road, while drivers
rarely do. In a naturalistic drive of about an hour, with participants reading on a laptop in
the back seat, the sickness-relevant motion was very slow: longitudinal accelerations
peaked between 0.01 and 0.04 Hz and lateral ones between 0.01 and 0.1 Hz, with minor
vertical motion. Our inference is that on-screen cues should follow slow, smooth changes
in the car's motion.
This gives designers two levers: what surrounds the screen (what the eyes can still see
around the video) and the content itself (how images move on the screen). Most of the
evidence addresses the first lever. The second has less evidence but some strong results.

3. Key studies
Kuiper, Bos & Diels (2018): where the screen sits
Setup: 18 participants in the front passenger seat did a 15-minute slalom with a visual task
on a display, placed either at windscreen height (a peripheral view of the outside) or low
at glove-compartment height.
Finding: the windscreen-height display caused less carsickness.
For us: a rear screen cannot move without hardware, but the image can imitate the effect.
Keep the video high and use an edge strip as a moving horizon (our inference, not a finding
of the study).
TU/e Mobility Lab (Karjanto, Yusof et al., 2018 and 2019)
Setup: four systems gave passengers peripheral information about upcoming maneuvers,
two visual and two haptic. In the 2018 paper, an instrumented car on a real road behaved
like a fully automated car (a Wizard of Oz setup). In the Peripheral Visual Feedforward
System (PVFS), light moved from bottom to top on the right side to signal a right turn.
Page 4 of 13
Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing: Research Summary
Finding: PVFS improved situation awareness and lessened sickness without raising
mental workload, including for passengers watching a movie. The authors note that richer
information and active head movement may be needed in future work.
For us: a soft light that sweeps along the screen border and the ceiling toward the next
turn is a hardware-free translation.
Emond, Bohrmann & Zare (2024): review of visual cues
Setup: a review in Ergonomics (67(6), 772–800) of visual cueing systems meant to help
passengers anticipate the forces of car travel.
Finding: visual cues can mitigate sickness in some in-vehicle configurations, and results
tended to be more promising in peripheral vision than in central vision alone. The authors
name interactive screens and ambient lighting as promising routes and extended reality
as a direction for further research. The effect on situation awareness is still unclear, and
cues may help acceptance of highly automated vehicles. Their related experiment
(Applied Ergonomics, 123, 104387) combined dynamic light that supports the perception
of longitudinal motion with relaxing music, perfume, and ventilation in a simulated
passenger drive, so it cannot isolate light alone.
For us: the strongest overarching reference. It supports prioritizing screen-edge graphics
and ambient-light experiments, keeps extended reality as an optional future path, and
reminds us that reducing sickness and improving awareness are separate outcomes.
Korea University: RideVR and OnVehicleVR (2022 to 2024)
Setup: VR headsets in moving vehicles. RideVR mixed the car's motion into the content as
a changing background scene or a particle flow. OnVehicleVR integrated the linear and
circular optical flow of the real motion into the VR background.
Finding: sickness dropped significantly but was not eliminated, with no loss of immersion
in two experiments (a car and a ship). A follow-up with an active task compared simple
particles with overlaid video from a car-mounted camera. The camera overlay did not
significantly reduce sickness, and the authors suspect that shaky, high-frequency
footage at a low frame rate cancelled the benefit.
For us: these are headset studies, so we use them as principles only: avoid raw road
footage and use simple, steady motion flow.
Page 5 of 13
Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing: Research Summary
4. Design rules
1. Simple beats literal. Abstract particles or light work better than shaky, low-frame-rate
road footage (Korea University).
2. Put motion flow in the border or background. Place the car's motion around the
content, not on top of it (Korea University; Nissan, 2008, which shifted the image with
vehicle and head motion and added clues separating the image's own motion from the
correction).
3. Keep cues slow and smooth. The car's sickness-relevant motion is slow (TU Delft,
2025), and shaky, fast motion cancelled the benefit in the camera-overlay test. This is
our reading, not a tested rule.
4. Test timing, do not assume it. Anticipatory cues are supported by Karjanto et al. and
Kuiper et al. (2020), but 15 lighting professionals in a workshop preferred cues played
at the same time as the maneuver, and logarithmic over linear speed scaling for light
dots. That is expert preference, not measured sickness.
5. Match speed, but not too loosely. Synced content reduces sickness but limits what
can be shown, and content slower than the real speed felt strange.
6. Tailor cues and keep them optional. The light-cue results conflict, but both studies
suggest the effect is strongest for sensitive riders. In the TU Delft study, everyone very
prone to carsickness was also very prone to visually induced sickness, so
susceptibility behaves like a personal trait. One cited hypothesis (Bijveld et al.) is that
some people use visual cues well and others do not, which may explain why some
prefer a view of the road and others close their eyes.
5. Shipped examples
Apple Vehicle Motion Cues (iOS 18, 2024). Animated dots at the screen edges shift with
turns, move down when the car accelerates, and move up when it brakes, designed to
reduce sensory conflict without interfering with the main content. The public evidence is
thin: one reviewer reported a large reduction but noted that stationary dots can get in the
way of maps, text, and images on straight roads. A 2026 real-road study exists, but the
abstract we saw reports no results.
holoride. This system syncs VR content to the car's telemetry (turns, acceleration,
braking). Audi reported at CES that far more participants than expected stayed free of
sickness. That is a company claim, not independent evidence.
Page 6 of 13
Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing: Research Summary
6. Three design directions to prototype
Three directions come out of the research, ordered by how well the evidence supports
them.
Direction A: predictive peripheral motion graphics (recommended first). Keep the
movie or central image unchanged and add a restrained graphic layer at the screen edges
that signals an upcoming maneuver: a left or right cue before a turn, and a distinct but
subtle cue before braking or acceleration. Compare no cue, an anticipatory cue, and a
reactive cue. Best fit: rear-seat movies, reading or working on a device, and automated-
vehicle passengers who are not looking at the road. Basis: Karjanto et al. (2018), consistent
with Kuiper et al. (2020).
Direction B: motion-aware video and visual flow. Make three edits of the same short clip:
(1) conventional editing and camera movement; (2) a relatively stable horizon, fewer
abrupt cuts, and reduced visual movement; (3) abstract graphics whose direction or
timing follows simulated vehicle motion. Keep content and duration as consistent as
possible. Best fit: immersive entertainment, video playback, and motion-design
experiments where the screen is the object of study. Evidence status: a design hypothesis
informed by sensory-conflict research, not a proven treatment. A calmer video may feel
more comfortable without reducing physiological sickness, and exaggerated or
mismatched motion could make symptoms worse.
Direction C: ambient light that anticipates movement. Design a light sequence around or
behind the rear screen and compare constant light, a cue that begins before movement,
and a cue that responds after it. Vary onset timing, direction, brightness, and duration
while keeping the overall light level comfortable. Best fit: enclosed passenger capsules,
shared cabins, and cases where screen overlays would distract from the content.
Evidence status: promising, but not isolated in the combined-intervention study, so treat
specific patterns as concepts to test.

screen turns without interrupting the
movie?

7. Where it fits in Direction 01
In Direction 01 the research attaches to one moment, Move with User: Alex gives
Margaret the front seat and moves to the back, and his video continues on the rear screen
from the same second. This is the rear-screen case the research covers.
Persona Role in the motion layer
Alex (21) The main fit. His comfort setting travels with him
like his music and his color.
Mina (8 months) No screen, so no cues. Nothing from Alex's cues
reaches her dimmed, breathing zone.
Margaret (72) A guest with no stored profile, so no motion layer
by default. Her screen stays the one-question card.
Emily (driver) Sees none of it. Cues stay in the rear zones, short
and calm, so they never distract the driver at night.
None of our personas is described as motion-sensitive, so the layer should be an opt-in
comfort setting in Alex's profile, not a diagnosis. The TU Delft study suggests a low-cost
way to set it: ask the passenger to fill in the short susceptibility questionnaire (MSSQ-
short) when the profile is created, instead of inferring sensitivity from the camera. In that
study the questionnaire correlated with on-road carsickness between 0.30 and 0.60
across the car measures, although other studies reported weaker correlations.
Page 8 of 13
Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing: Research Summary
8. Four experiments for 4D/5D and Motion
All four use only screens, light, and motion and can be driven from a hand-made file of
acceleration values, so no hardware is needed. Experiment 1 combines Directions A and C,
experiments 2 and 3 are Direction B, and experiment 4 tests our own personal-zone logic.

9. How to test
Use the same base footage or simulated motion in every condition so the main difference
is the visual treatment. A classroom video or simulator study can assess perceived
comfort and cue legibility, but it cannot prove effectiveness in real driving.
Page 9 of 13
Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing: Research Summary
Keep exposures short, let anyone stop at once, and never try to provoke strong
symptoms.
Before and after each condition, rate nausea, dizziness, and general discomfort.
Ask whether participants noticed and understood the cue, and whether it was
distracting or confusing.
For video conditions, also ask about comfort, immersion, and willingness to continue
watching.
Record baseline susceptibility with the short questionnaire (MSSQ-short). Some
people barely react to sickening motion and others react quickly, which hides
differences between conditions, so include people who get carsick.
Keep motion, duration, screen size, viewing distance, and content as consistent as
possible, and keep the cue motion slow and smooth.
Measure symptoms, situation awareness, and perceived comfort as separate
outcomes.
Compare cues that start before the maneuver with cues that start after it.
10. Conclusion
The most defensible starting point is predictive peripheral motion graphics. The Emond et
al. review reinforces that visual cues are more promising in peripheral vision and points to
interactive screens and ambient lighting as useful directions, while direct in-car studies
provide precedents for anticipatory peripheral cues and for preserving peripheral visual
information. Motion-aware video remains a strong creative avenue for 4D/5D or Motion
classes, but its benefit is an open question. The best next step is a controlled comparison
of no cue, an anticipatory peripheral cue, and a reactive cue, followed by a separate
ambient-light or motion-aware-video experiment.
The central design opportunity is to treat the rear screen not only as entertainment but
as a visual interface between the passenger and the moving vehicle. The best design may
not be the most animated one: it may be a subtle, well-timed cue that improves
anticipation without taking the passenger out of the experience.
11. Limits of the evidence
We read abstracts and summaries, not full papers, so check every citation and number
against the paper before putting it on a slide.
Sample sizes are small, usually 16 to 32 participants, and the light-cue results conflict.
Page 10 of 13
Motion Comfort for Rear-Screen Viewing: Research Summary
The Korea University studies used VR headsets, so their findings are design principles,
not proof for rear screens.
Studies that combine light with music, perfume, and ventilation cannot isolate the
effect of light alone, and the Emond et al. review notes limits in how well results
transfer across vehicle configurations.
The Apple and holoride results are anecdotal or company-reported.
The TU Delft study had 22 participants, measured susceptibility rather than any
mitigation, and used a reading task, not video. It cites another study (Metzulat et al.,
2024) reporting somewhat more sickness with a dynamic visual task than with an
auditory one, which hints that video may be more provocative than reading; we have
not read that study. The questionnaire's predictive power also varies: 0.30 to 0.60 in
that study, but about 0.2 to 0.3 in earlier work it cites for passive driving.
The Smyth et al. training study concerns visuospatial ability, not screen design, and we
should not expect similar reductions from any visual design.
We found no strong evidence specific to braking and acceleration cues, so treat those
as hypotheses.
The prototype assumes the car's motion data is available. Confirm this with the team.

## C34. Cabin Safety Concept (docx)
oktyabr · mənbə: fc4f346b-Cabin_Safety_Concept.txt
Cabin Safety Concept: Weapon Scan and AI Emergency Call
Team A · Contextual Mobility UX · Design Narrative · October 2026
Overview
Ideas 1 and 2 run on the existing interior camera, ambient lighting, and screens, as the brief requires. They extend Direction 01 (Passenger-Centered Personalization), where the car already knows who sits where and each person has a personal zone of light and screen.
Idea 1: Cabin scan for weapons (violence prevention)
1. What do people see? When someone sits down, a soft scanning light sweeps across their seat in their personal color, like a gentle wave. If all is clear, the light settles into a calm glow. If the camera's AI recognizes a visible weapon, the light near that seat turns amber and a short alert appears on the center screen, visible to the driver only.
2. When does it happen? At boarding, and again if someone new enters or an item appears mid-ride.
3. Who is it for? Margaret (72), who feels vulnerable in a car at night and wouldn't say so, and Emily and Daniel, who are driving and can't watch the back seat.
4. Why does it matter? Safety becomes visible and calm. Nobody has to confront anyone or spot the danger themselves, and the scanning wave tells the family the car is looking out for them.
Idea 2: AI emergency call
1. What do people see? If the camera's AI detects suspicious or violent behavior, the cabin light pulses red and the screen shows a large "Calling for help?" button with a countdown. If the driver taps it, or the system is highly confident and nobody responds, the car sends its location to the police and the screen shows "Help is on the way."
2. When does it happen? After Idea 1's warning, or when behavior inside the car escalates.
3. Who is it for? The driver, who can't reach a phone while driving, and Margaret, who needs one big button instead of menus.
4. Why does it matter? The car can reach help when the people inside can't. The countdown lets an adult cancel a false alarm before the police are called.
Idea 3: Self-diagnosing car
The car checks its own condition and tells the driver in plain visuals when a part needs attention, using the sensors and diagnostics cars already have, so no new hardware is added.
1. What do people see? A small car outline on the center screen highlights the part that needs attention: amber for "fix soon," red for "stop safely." One plain sentence explains it, such as "Rear left tire pressure is low." The ambient light near the driver pulses once in the same color, then settles. Alerts stay out of the passengers' and sleeping baby's zones unless the problem is safety-critical.
2. When does it happen? At the start of each ride, as a short "all clear" check, and mid-ride when the car detects a part changing condition.
3. Who is it for? Emily and Daniel, who drive and rarely notice slowly developing problems, and Margaret (72), who can't read dashboard warning symbols.
4. Why does it matter? Problems are caught before they become breakdowns, which matters most on a night drive with a baby on board. Plain language replaces cryptic dashboard icons, so nobody has to guess what a warning light means.
Scenario: one situation, three personas
Situation: a night drive. Daniel stops on a dark street to pick up Ethan, and the camera detects a person with a visible weapon approaching the car.
Mina (8 months), asleep
What she experiences: her zone stays dimmed, and no alert flashes or sounds at her seat.
Value: her sleep is protected, since alerts go only to the adults.
Alex (21)
What he experiences: amber light at his seat and a message on the center screen. He taps to confirm the call.
Value: he acts as a second pair of eyes without panicking the family.
Margaret (72)
What she experiences: one large red button on her screen and a calm voice saying "Help is being called."
Value: she can act with no menus and no fear of doing it wrong.
Differentiated value
Safety: earlier awareness and faster help.
Autonomy: a human can cancel before the call is sent.
Emotional: calm light signals instead of panic.
Privacy: all recognition runs inside the car and no footage is stored.

## C37. 11/2 skripti (KR)
2 noyabr · mənbə: bbb69c0e-Script_11-2_KR.txt
Seats That Know You · 11/2 발표 Script
Team A · 약 4–5분
01 · Title
안녕하세요, Team A입니다. 이번 주에는 Light design, Camera prototype, 그리고 Design
system을 중심으로 작업했습니다. 지금부터 결과를 보여드리겠습니다.
02 · 이번 주 진행 내용
먼저 이번 주에 진행한 내용을 간단히 정리하면, 조명의 위치를 정하고, 각 상태에서 빛이 어떻게
움직이는지를 Light spec으로 정리했습니다. 이렇게 미리 정해 두면 이후 애니메이션과 화면 디
자인 작업을 할 때 더 정확하게 맞출 수 있습니다. 그리고 Camera prototype을 테스트했고,
Design system 초안과 Welcome screen 초기 스케치도 만들었습니다.
03 · 4개의 Light zone
다음은 조명 위치입니다. 보시는 것처럼 Door lines, Screen halo, Console lines, Under-seat
glow, 이렇게 네 개의 영역에 조명을 배치했습니다. 위치를 정할 때 가장 중요하게 생각한 것은 운
전자에게 방해가 되지 않는 것이었기 때문에, 천장이나 운전자 시야 쪽에는 조명을 두지 않았습니
다. 그리고 창문 아래 라인은 앞문과 이어지도록 해서, 자리를 바꾸거나 콘텐츠를 공유할 때 빛이
좌석 사이로 이동하는 모습이 잘 보이도록 했습니다.
04 · Color system
이어서 컬러 시스템입니다. 저희 시스템에서는 색이 좌석이 아니라 사람을 따라갑니다. 한 탑승자
의 모든 영역이 그 사람의 색으로 켜지고, 자리를 바꾸면 색도 함께 이동합니다. Teal, Lavender,
Coral은 색각 이상이 있어도 구분할 수 있는 색이라서 선택했고, 경고의 의미가 있는 빨간색과 초
록색은 사용하지 않았습니다. 이미지에서는 밝게 보이지만, 실제로는 은은하게 켜져 있다가 이벤트
가 있을 때만 약 2초 정도 밝아집니다.
05 · Light spec: Welcome
다음은 Light spec의 예시인 Welcome입니다. 문이 열리면 Door lines가 먼저 켜지고, 탑승자가
앉으면 Under-seat glow, 인식이 되면 Screen halo가 켜진 뒤 다시 안정된 상태로 돌아갑니다.
나머지 상태들도 같은 규칙으로 정리되어 있습니다.
06 · Camera prototype
다음으로 Camera prototype입니다. 테스트 결과 9번 중 8번 정확하게 인식했고, 실패한 한 번
도 다른 사람의 이름이 아니라 Guest로 표시했습니다. 두 명을 등록했을 때 한 번 잠깐 혼동이 있
었지만 바로 수정되었고, 인식 거리는 약 1.5m까지 가능했습니다. 다만 진한 메이크업이나 어두운
조명에서는 인식이 약해지기 때문에, 다음 단계에서는 사용자마다 여러 모습을 등록하려고 합니다.
07 · Design system
이어서 Design system 초안입니다. 사용자 컬러 세 가지와 시스템 컬러 세 가지가 있고, 폰트는
영문과 한글을 함께 쓸 수 있는 Pretendard로 정했습니다. 화면 크기는 실제 차량을 참고해서
11.6인치와 12.3인치로 정했습니다.
08 · Welcome screen
이것은 Welcome screen의 초기 스케치입니다. 글자 크기와 레이아웃은 After Effects에서 애
니메이션을 만들면서 더 다듬을 예정입니다.
09 · 다음 주 계획
마지막으로 다음 주에는 Figma에서 화면들과 Cabin Map을 디자인하고, Welcome animation
작업을 시작하며, Prototype v2를 만들어 사용자 테스트를 진행할 예정입니다. 감사합니다.

## C38. Hyundai research summary (Alina)
7 noyabr · mənbə: c5803658-hyundai_research_summary.txt
Hyundai ccNC and Seon Design System — Research
Summary
1. System Overview
The Hyundai system most relevant to our project is ccNC (connected car Navigation Cockpit) and its
design system, Seon (■).
Seon means “Line” in Korean. The design system uses horizontal lines and simple linear elements as its
main visual motif. Hyundai developed this system from small graphic components to the entire display,
creating a simple and flat visual style.
An important characteristic of ccNC is that it uses the same grid system and common components across
different displays. This creates a consistent experience between the digital cluster and infotainment screens.
For our project, Hyundai's Seon design system is useful because it focuses on simple and flat graphics, clear
information hierarchy, consistency between multiple screens and good visibility while driving.
2. Typography — Hyundai Sans UI
Hyundai uses Hyundai Sans UI, a digital typeface specifically designed for mobility and user interfaces.
According to Hyundai, the typeface was developed to provide high legibility in different driving situations,
clear information hierarchy and readability in different environments.
For our Welcome screen, the typography hierarchy could be:
Main Welcome Message / User Name → Semibold or Bold
Main Information → Regular
Secondary Information → Regular or Light
Hyundai Sans UI is a Hyundai corporate typeface, so we should not assume that it is freely licensed for
general use unless an official license is provided. For our academic project, Pretendard can be used as a
practical working alternative because it is clean, highly readable and suitable for Korean and English UI text.
3. Color Direction
The exact HEX values for Hyundai's complete ccNC UI color system do not appear to be publicly available in
the official sources used for this research. Hyundai's design direction emphasizes dark themes, strong
visibility, simple flat graphics and restrained visual elements.
For our project, we can use these principles rather than claim unofficial colors as Hyundai's exact system.
Light Mode: light or white neutral background, dark text, lavender or coral as the personalized accent color.
Night Mode: dark neutral background, light text, lavender or coral as the personalized accent color.
Lavender and coral represent our project's personalization system, not official Hyundai UI colors. The accent
or edge color should remain on a separate animation layer so that it can easily be changed later.
4. Screen Sizes
Hyundai does not use exactly one physical screen size for every vehicle. However, 12.3-inch displays are a
common reference in ccNC-equipped vehicles, including configurations such as a 12.3-inch digital cluster
and a 12.3-inch navigation/infotainment display.
For our project, a 12.3-inch automotive display can be used as a main design reference while keeping the
interface flexible enough to adapt to other screen sizes.
The Seon design system is more important than copying one exact screen size because Hyundai uses a
shared grid and common components across multiple displays.
Conclusion and Application to Our Project
Design System: Hyundai ccNC — Seon (■)
Visual Style: Simple, flat, clear hierarchy, horizontal and linear visual elements, consistent layout and high
readability.
Typography Reference: Hyundai Sans UI
Working Alternative: Pretendard
Screen Reference: 12.3-inch automotive display
Color System: Light and night modes with lavender and coral as personalized accent colors.
For the Welcome animation, the main message and important information should remain close to the center
of the composition. This will make it easier to adapt the animation to a vertical 9:16 mobile version as well as
a wide automotive display.
Overall, Hyundai's Seon system is a useful reference because it provides a consistent design language
across different vehicle displays while maintaining simple graphics and clear readability for drivers.
References
1. Hyundai Motor — Seon Design System & Hyundai Sans UI:
https://www.hyundai.com/content/hyundai/worldwide/en/newsroom/detail/0000000287.html
2. Hyundai Motor Group — ccNC, Seon and Hyundai Sans UI:
https://www.hyundaimotorgroup.com/ko/story/CONT0000000000123119
3. Hyundai Motor Group — ccNC Design and Dark Theme:
https://www.hyundaimotorgroup.com/ko/story/CONT0000000000133518

## C39. App–vehicle connection & facial recognition research (Alina), bərpa olunmuş mətn
7 noyabr · mənbə: app_vehicle_research.txt
Research Summary: App–Vehicle Connection and FacialRecognition
1. How the App Could Connect to the Car
For our concept, the most realistic solution would be a smartphone app that connects to the vehicle usingtechnologies that already exist in current cars.A useful reference is Hyundai Digital Key 2. Hyundai already allows users to register their smartphone as adigital key and communicate with the vehicle using Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE), Ultra-Wideband (UWB), andNFC. BLE can be used for remote functions, while UWB allows the vehicle to recognize when an authorizedsmartphone is nearby and enables proximity-based interaction.This suggests that our app could work in a similar way. The user would first create a personal profile in theapp. When they approach their vehicle, the phone and car could recognize each other through Bluetoothand/or UWB. The vehicle would then connect to the user's profile and activate their personalized settings.Recommended system:Phone App → Bluetooth/UWB connection → Vehicle recognizes the user's profile → In-car system activatespersonalized settings.Real example: Hyundai Digital Key 2 uses BLE, UWB and NFC for smartphone-to-vehicle interaction.
2. Facial Recognition: App Registration and In-Car Verification
The facial recognition process could be divided into two stages. First, the user could register their facethrough the smartphone app, creating a personal biometric profile connected to their account. Then, when theuser enters the vehicle, an in-car camera could verify the person and activate the correct personalized profile.However, the system should not simply copy Apple's Face ID technology. Face ID uses specialized hardwareand sensors, so a normal vehicle camera would not necessarily work in exactly the same way. Instead, ourconcept could use the phone for initial enrollment and the vehicle camera for identity verification.Basic process:1. The user creates a profile in the app.2. The user voluntarily enrolls their facial information.3. The smartphone connects to the vehicle through Bluetooth/UWB.4. The in-car camera verifies the person.5. The vehicle activates the user's personalized comfort settings.A relevant example comes from Hyundai's privacy documentation, which states that biometric features maybe used at the user's request and with consent, and that certain biometric information can be storedexclusively on the vehicle.
3. Data Storage
The vehicle could have a local infotainment or computing system with significant storage capacity, forexample 128–256 GB. However, this does not mean that one person's facial data would require hundreds ofmegabytes. A facial recognition system can use a biometric or facial template rather than permanently storinglarge numbers of original photographs.The vehicle's storage would mainly be used for the vehicle operating system, infotainment and software,personalized user profiles, comfort settings, temporary vehicle data and other system information.Recommended approach: The user can choose to save their profile locally in a personal car. Sensitivefacial information should be processed locally whenever possible. Cloud services could be used mainly for
account synchronization or transferring settings between authorized devices rather than constantly storing ortransmitting facial images.
4. Could the System Work for Taxis?
Technically, a similar system could be developed for taxis, but a full facial-recognition system does not seemsuitable for existing taxi fleets.For the system to work in every taxi, existing vehicles would need additional hardware such as interiorcameras, facial recognition software, compatible computing systems, secure storage and connections to thetaxi platform. Installing this equipment in a large number of existing taxis would be expensive andcomplicated. It would also create additional privacy concerns because a taxi is used by many differentpassengers every day.Therefore, our conclusion is that the full system is more suitable for privately owned vehicles or future smartvehicles that are designed with the necessary hardware from the beginning. The technology could potentiallybe introduced to future premium or smart mobility services, but retrofitting all existing taxis with facialrecognition hardware would probably not be practical.Conclusion:Future smart taxi: Possible if the technology is integrated during vehicle design.Existing taxi fleet: Technically possible, but expensive and impractical because it would require large-scalehardware installation.
5. Privacy
Facial information is highly sensitive because it can be used to identify a specific person. Therefore, thesystem should be based on user consent and data minimization.Our recommended privacy approach is:\177 Facial recognition is optional.\177 The user gives clear consent before enrolling biometric information.\177 Sensitive data is processed locally in the vehicle whenever possible.\177 The system stores only the information necessary for verification and personalization.\177 The user can delete their profile and biometric information at any time.\177 Facial data should not automatically be shared with unnecessary third parties.Hyundai provides a useful real-world example by describing biometric features that are provided at the user'srequest and with explicit consent, with certain biometric data stored on the vehicle until the user deletes theinformation or resets the system.
Final Recommendation
The most suitable system for our project would be:1. Personal Profile in the Phone AppThe user creates a personal account and saves their comfort preferences.2. Automatic Vehicle ConnectionThe smartphone connects to the vehicle through Bluetooth Low Energy and/or UWB when the userapproaches.3. Profile Recognition and In-Car VerificationThe vehicle recognizes the authorized user's profile, while an interior camera verifies the user and activatesthe correct personalized settings.4. Privacy Control
Biometric data is optional, securely stored locally when possible, and can be deleted by the user.Overall, this system is most realistic for privately owned vehicles and future smart vehicles. Existing taxi fleetsare less suitable because adding cameras and biometric hardware to every vehicle would require expensiveand large-scale modifications.
References
1. Hyundai Motor Company — Digital Key 2 (Official Korea):https://www.hyundai.com/kr/ko/digital-customer-support/app/myhyundai/hyundai-digital-key-2/information
2. Hyundai Motor Company — Digital Key 2 User Guide: https://www.hyundai.com/content/dam/hyundai/au/en/owning/digital-key/ioniq-9/Hyundai-Digital-Key-2-User-Guide-V1.1.pdf
3. Hyundai Owner's Manual — Smartphone Digital Key:https://ownersmanual.hyundai.com/full_webhelp/DN8HEV/2026/ko_KR/idac62131fcbf.html
4. Hyundai USA — Vehicle Technologies & Service Privacy Notice:https://www.hyundaiusa.com/us/en/technologies-services-privacy-page
5. Samsung Newsroom — Samsung Wallet Digital Key for Mercedes-Benz: https://news.samsung.com/kr/

## C40. Share / music in car research summary (Azhar)
7 noyabr · mənbə: fb00b98a-share_music_car_research_summary.txt
SHARE + MUSIC IN THE CAR
Research summary and concept recommendation | 6 November 2026
Core recommendation: Make Share a car-wide social experience—not another form of phone mirroring. The app should connect people,
screens and ambient lighting, while existing music services handle playback wherever their rules allow.
1. SHARE — What can the car do better than a phone?
What exists. Chevrolet’s passenger-entertainment system lets a rear passenger swipe video towards another screen or press Share; the
receiving passenger accepts or declines. Some rear-seat systems can mirror one source across two displays. This proves that screen-to-screen
sharing is practical.
What remains inconvenient. People may need to pass a phone around, connect cables, sign in separately, find the same content again, or
expose personal notifications and accounts. A phone-to-car connection does not automatically create a shared space where several
passengers can contribute.
Our opportunity. Create a shared vehicle session: send a photo, video or playlist to a chosen seat/screen; show an accept/decline request;
offer synchronized “watch together” on multiple passenger screens; keep personal screens and accounts private; and let passengers
contribute to one shared queue. Keep video and distracting interactions unavailable to the driver while moving.
2. MUSIC — One shared experience, different services
Existing examples. Apple Music SharePlay works with CarPlay: the driver starts playback and passengers join from iPhones by notification or
QR code, then contribute to the session. Spotify Jam supports in-person sessions in Android Auto and CarPlay: passengers join by QR code or
Bluetooth and add, skip or rearrange songs from their phones. BMW’s YouTube Music integration supports separate accounts on driver,
passenger and rear displays; any logged-in display can control the car audio. Each example, however, remains tied to its own service.
Can our app combine Apple Music, Spotify and YouTube Music? Partly. Apple Music offers MusicKit for catalogue access, account
authorization and playback. Spotify offers APIs and playback control, but its current developer policy prohibits commercial streaming
integrations, non-interactive broadcasting, integration with streams from another service, and synchronizing Spotify recordings with visual
media. YouTube Music has no equivalent public third-party playback SDK that makes a universal player straightforward. A single in-app player
mixing all three is therefore not a safe assumption.
When people use different apps. Share the track identity and a service-specific link—not the audio itself. Match by title, artist and, where
available, recording identifiers; search the receiver’s preferred service; then open the matching track there. If no match exists, show the original
link or let the person choose another version. Region, subscription, catalogue and live/remix versions can prevent an exact match.
3. OUR APP + SCREENS + LIGHT — How it fits together
Use the app as the connection and session layer, not as a new streaming platform. Passengers connect supported music accounts once
through each provider’s official authorization flow. Store secure authorization tokens, never passwords, and let users disconnect. On entering
the car, they join a vehicle session and choose whether to keep content private or contribute to the shared space. The car interface manages
seat-to-seat sharing, permissions and the shared queue; playback is handed off to the relevant native service or supported integration.
Lighting should communicate social state, not just decorate the cabin. A seat colour can identify who shared a track; a gentle light
transition can show sender-to-recipient connection; and a shared-session state can blend participants’ colours. Keep cues peripheral, subtle
and adjustable. Never rely on colour alone—add a screen label or icon for accessibility.
4. What the research papers add
Visual cues and motion sickness — Emond, Bohrmann & Zare (2024). Their review finds that visual cues can reduce motion sickness in
some vehicle configurations and tend to work better in peripheral vision than only in central vision. Their effect on situational awareness
remains unclear. This supports exploring ambient light and peripheral cues, but does not prove that any animated lighting pattern prevents
sickness. Keep effects gentle and test them with passengers.
FabriCar — Khorsandi et al. (DIS 2023). The study compared embroidered e-textile media controls in the steering wheel, seat-belt pad and
headrest with screen-based controls, using 16 participants. The authors report fewer collisions and a 302.7% decrease in eye distraction. Treat
that as the paper’s reported figure, not a guaranteed real-world effect. Participants valued intuitive, reachable tactile gestures. Limitations
include simulator rather than real-car testing, no passenger testing, and unresolved durability/sensor-signal issues. Design implication:
consider tactile controls in armrests, seat surfaces or door panels for volume, play/pause and accepting a share.
Ambient light and well-being — Braun et al. (2024). An exploratory vehicle study combined guided breathing with LED light. Only six complete
datasets were available: four participants reported lower stress, two reported higher stress, and mean perceived stress decreased by 0.28 on a
five-point scale. This is preliminary, not proof of a clinical effect. It suggests an optional calm/comfort mode with adjustable light and breathing
guidance.
Ambient light as glanceable communication — Bhathad (2025). A blind-spot prototype used a left/right light gradient to communicate
proximity in peripheral vision, aiming to inform rather than interrupt. It was demonstrated in simulation and had not yet undergone formal user
SHARE + MUSIC IN THE CAR • 1
evaluation. The transferable principle is ambient cues as low-effort communication—not evidence that our social-lighting concept is already
validated.
5. Recommended direction and feasible first version
• Build Share first: send-to-screen, accept/decline, and synchronized passenger viewing where the content provider permits it.
• Build a service-neutral shared music queue second: share track metadata and links, then open the match in each passenger’s preferred
service. Start with native Spotify Jam / Apple SharePlay hand-off rather than promising one universal player.
• Connect the app to seat identity, screen permissions and lighting. Use light for sender/receiver/session feedback, with brightness controls
and non-colour labels.
• Prototype with one music provider first, then test cross-service matching separately. Validate latency, catalogue availability, privacy,
comfort and driver distraction.
Bottom line: The strongest differentiator is not “all music apps in one player.” It is a passenger-centred sharing layer that makes screens,
tactile controls and ambient lighting work together. Cross-platform music can begin as metadata/link hand-off and grow only where provider
permissions and APIs allow.
Sources
• Emond, Bohrmann & Zare (2024), motion-sickness visual-cues review — DOI 10.1080/00140139.2023.2286187
• Khorsandi et al. (2023), FabriCar — DOI 10.1145/3563657.3595988
• Bhathad (2025), ambient-light blind-spot system — DOI 10.1145/3744335.3762328
• Braun et al. (2024), immersive vehicle and mental well-being — DOI 10.54941/ahfe1005260
• Apple Support — SharePlay with CarPlay
• Spotify — Jam in Android Auto and CarPlay
• Google / BMW — YouTube Music multi-screen experience
• Chevrolet — Share video between passenger screens
• Apple Developer — MusicKit
• Spotify Developer Policy
SHARE + MUSIC IN THE CAR • 2

## C43. 11/9 skripti (KR), final
9 noyabr · mənbə: script_11-9_KR.txt
1 · Cover (~10초)
안녕하세요, A팀입니다. 오늘은 저희 concept을 처음부터 한 번 정리하고, 이번 주 작업과 앞으로의 plan을 말씀드리겠습니다.
2 · Concept (~1분 30초)
저희 프로젝트는 “Seats That Know You”입니다. 지금 차는 운전석은 운전자에게 맞춰 주지만, 뒷좌석이나 옆자리에 누가 앉았는지는 모릅니다. 그런데 한 차 안에 있는 사람들은 원하는 것이 다 다르죠. 저희는 차가 좌석마다 그 사람을 알아보게 하고 싶었습니다. 먼저 app에서 profile을 한 번 만들고, 자기 color를 고르고, 얼굴을 등록합니다. 차에 타면 실내 camera가 누가 어느 자리에 앉았는지 인식하고, 그 좌석의 light zone이 그 사람의 color로 켜지고, 앞 screen에는 그 사람의 content가 나옵니다. 주행 중에도 camera는 계속 그 사람을 보고 있어서, screen과 light가 그 사람과 계속 interaction합니다. 예를 들어 잠들면 screen이 꺼지고 light가 낮아집니다. Sound는 좌석마다 따로라서, 내 소리는 내 자리에서만 들립니다. 그리고 motion sickness가 있는 사람에게는 screen이 앞으로 올 커브나 정지 같은 차의 움직임을 미리 보여 줍니다. 자리를 바꾸면 설정이 따라가고, 다른 차에서도 profile이 이어집니다. 처음 탄 사람은 guest로 Champagne color를 받고 아무것도 저장되지 않습니다. 운전자는 center screen의 Cabin Map으로 뒷좌석을 한눈에 봅니다.
3 · Process (~30초)
그동안 feedback에 따라 방향을 좁혀 왔습니다. 지문 인식은 실내 camera로, scope는 2+2 좌석과 계정 기반 profile로 정리했고, 지난주 feedback으로 Hyundai system에 맞는 screen, color, app 연결, narrative를 이번 주에 작업했습니다.
4 · 이번 주에 한 것 (~15초)
이번 주에는 narrative, color system, camera prototype v2, car screen 구조, 그리고 app, 이렇게 다섯 가지를 했습니다.
5 · Narrative (~1분 15초)
이 concept이 실제 한 순간에 어떻게 작동하는지 보여 주기 위해 한 명의 persona를 정했습니다. Sofia는 36살 marketing manager이고, 매일 퇴근 후 두 아이를 데려옵니다. Leo는 여섯 살로 뒷좌석 오른쪽, Mia는 두 살로 왼쪽의 뒤를 보는 car seat에 앉습니다. 집까지 16분 정도 걸립니다. Sofia의 문제는 뒤를 돌아보지 않으면 아이들 상태를 알 수 없다는 겁니다. Monash 대학 연구에 따르면 아이들은 휴대폰보다 운전자의 주의를 12배 더 빼앗고, 16분 주행 중 약 3분은 도로를 보지 않는데, 대부분 뒤나 mirror를 보는 시간입니다. 여기서 저희 concept이 그대로 작동합니다. 차에 타면 camera가 Leo와 Mia를 알아보고, 각자 자리가 자기 color로 켜집니다. Leo의 cartoon sound는 Leo 자리에서만 들립니다. Mia가 잠들면 camera가 이를 알아보고, Mia의 zone이 조용한 breathing light로 바뀌어서 Sofia는 mirror로 1초 만에 알 수 있습니다. Sofia는 Cabin Map으로도 확인할 수 있고, 차를 세우면 뒤에 아이가 남아 있다고 알려 줍니다. 같은 system이 친구나 guest가 탈 때도 똑같이 작동합니다.
6 · Color System (~45초)
기본 color는 Champagne 하나이고 guest도 이 color를 받습니다. 개인 color는 Hyundai mood light 계열에서 차분한 톤 8개를 골라 저희 palette로 만들었습니다. 한 사람의 모든 light zone은 그 사람의 color로 켜지고, 색을 구분하기 어려운 분들을 위해 color만이 아니라 위치, 이름, motion으로도 알려 줍니다.
7 · Camera Prototype v2 (~1분)
이번 주 camera prototype입니다. 등록된 얼굴을 알아보면 이름과 Welcome message를 보여 줍니다. 두 좌석을 지원해서 왼쪽 사람은 왼쪽 screen에, 오른쪽 사람은 오른쪽 screen에 나오고, 자리를 바꾸면 screen도 바뀝니다. 눈을 감았다 뜨는 것도 인식해서, Mia 장면의 sleep 감지를 이걸로 test하고 있습니다.
8 · Screen & App Structure (~45초)
Screen은 세 단계입니다. 탈 때는 recognition, guest Welcome, 자리를 바꾸면 설정이 따라오는 화면. 주행 중 seat screen에는 personal home, sound zone, screen share, conflict, 그리고 camera interaction. 운전자 center screen에는 Cabin Map과 하차 전 reminder가 있습니다. App은 처음 profile을 만들고, 주행 후 ride summary를 보여 줍니다.
9 · Screen Sketches (~45초)
이건 지금 작업 중인 첫 screen sketch입니다. 왼쪽 두 개는 app으로, color 선택과 home 화면이고, 오른쪽은 seat screen으로, personal home과 자리를 바꿨을 때 설정이 따라오는 화면입니다. 지금은 design보다 function을 screen 위에서 구체화하는 단계이고, 이걸 먼저 demo로 만든 다음 design 작업을 해서 app과 screen design을 final로 완성할 계획입니다.
10 · Next Steps (~1분)
앞으로는 step by step으로 진행하려고 합니다. 먼저 camera로 할 수 있는 흥미로운 function을 계속 찾고 test하고 있습니다. 이 과정은 계속 이어 갈 거고요. Screen function이 camera가 할 수 있는 것과 연결되어 있기 때문에, camera 결과에 맞춰 screen 위에서 function을 구체화하고, 그걸 demo로 연결합니다. 그다음 screen animation과 함께 app과 screen design을 final로 완성하겠습니다. 감사합니다.

# Əlavə D. Fayl inventarı
"outputs": oktyabrda hazırlanmış fayllar (tarix fayl tarixidir). "uploads" və "attachments": Mina-nın yüklədiyi və ya söhbətə əlavə olunmuş fayllar (şəkillər, ekran görüntüləri, komanda sənədləri, başqa komandaların PDF-ləri: Team B, C, D, E). "8–9 noyabr işləri": app, ekran və təqdimat faylları. Fayl adlarındakı tarixlər (məs. Screenshot_2026-10-29) faylın yarandığı günü göstərir.
